Even these are the words which Jehovah hath said respecting</span> <span style="color:#000080">Israel and respecting Judah:</span>

<!-- PAGE 1294 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">5. Verily thus hath Jehovah said —  (The voice of trembling have</span> <span style="color:#000080">we heard, Of fear and not of peace:</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">6. Ask ye now and see, Does a man travail with child?  How is it? I</span> <span style="color:#000080">see every man</span> <span style="color:#000080">*With*</span> <span style="color:#000080">his hands on his loins like a woman in travail,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And turned are all faces to paleness: )</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">7. Hark! for great</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall be*</span> <span style="color:#000080">that day, none like it; Though a time of</span> <span style="color:#000080">distress shall be to Jacob, Yet from it shall he be saved:</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">8. And it shall be in that day, saith Jehovah of hosts, That I shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">break, etc.. etc..</span></p>

The parenthesis accounts for what is said at the end of the 7th verse (Jeremiah 30:7) and is intended as a contrast with the great day of deliverance that is promised. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF4</span>

I render the verse as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">8. And it shall be in that day, saith Jehovah of hosts,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*That*</span> <span style="color:#000080">I shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">break his yoke from thy neck, And thy chains will I burst: And</span> <span style="color:#000080">make him to serve shall strangers no more: 9. But serve shall they</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah, etc..</span>

The transition from the second to the third person, “thy” and “him,” and from the singular to the plural, “him” and “they,” is very common in the Prophets. On the last line in the 8th verse (Jeremiah 30:8). — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF5</span> The word is stronger than “fear;” it means to be broken down in mind, to be dispirited, so as to give up all hope. The distance, mentioned in the following clause, was calculated to dispirit them, and they feared lest their children should continue in bondage. Hence it is said, “Fear not,” that is, as to a final return; and “be not dispirited,” or disheartened, with respect to the distance. The order, as is commonly the case, is reversed. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF6</span> *Calvin* renders <span style="color:#008080">bç</span>, “dwell,” as though it came from <span style="color:#008080">bçy</span> but most render it “return,” as in our version. Then, “to be at rest,” and “in a quiet state,” are not sufficiently distinct. I render the clause thus, —

<!-- PAGE 1295 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And return shall Jacob and be at rest, And secure shall he be, and</span> <span style="color:#000080">none making him afraid.</span></p>

Security is freedom from disturbance: “he shall be prosperous,” as rendered by some, is by no means suitable. “Jacob,” being the father of the twelve patriarchs, is to be understood as including both Israel and Judah, according to the 4th verse (Jeremiah 30:4). — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF7</span> There is no verb in the first clause, “Because I with thee.” The context shews that the future is meant; then the rendering ought to be, “Because I *shall be* with thee;” that is, at the restoration of the people to their own land, mentioned in the preceding verse. So *Calvin* understood the clause, though the early versions, like our own, gave the verb in the present tense, which is by no means correct. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF8</span> This clause is rendered by the *Vulg.,* “that thou mayest not seem to thyself innocent;” by the *Syr.,* “but I will not suffer thee to be wholly unpunished;” and by the *Targ.,* “and destroying I will not destroy thee.” Both *Venema* and *Blayney* follow the meaning of the *Targum;* the later version is, “And will not make thee altogether desolate.” The phrase occurs in Jeremiah 25:29, and also in Exodus 34:7; Numbers 14:18; Nahum 1:3; in which places the idea of the verb is, to hold guiltless or innocent, to acquit, to let go unpunished, and not to make desolate, to cut off or to destroy. That the former is its meaning in Exodus 34:7, is evident from the explanation which follows, “holding guiltless he will not hold guiltless, visiting the iniquity of the fathers,” etc.; visiting the fathers’ iniquity proves that it is not held guiltless or suffered to go unpunished. The verb, <span style="color:#008080">hqn</span> means to be free, or to count one free, from pollution, crime, guilt, or punishment. To let free from punishment, is the idea most suitable here; God would chastise them in some measure, and would not suffer them to be wholly unpunished. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF9</span> The <span style="color:#008080">l</span> in Hebrew is sometimes the, <span style="color:#0000d4">κατὰ</span> in Greek; it means, as to, with regard to; so here, — <span style="color:#000080">Incurable as to thy bruise, Grievous</span> <span style="color:#000080">*has been*</span> <span style="color:#000080">thy stroke.</span>

Or we may give this rendering, inverting the order, — <span style="color:#000080">As to thy bruise,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*it is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">incurable; Grievous</span> <span style="color:#000080">*has been*</span> <span style="color:#000080">thy stroke.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1296 -->

The “bruise” occasioned by the “grievous stroke” was incurable, that is, by human means. The effect is mentioned first, “the bruise;” then the cause, “the stroke.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF10</span> According to *Calvin,* the verse reads thus, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">No one undertakes thy cause; For a cure, medicines and healing</span> <span style="color:#000080">thou hast not.</span></p>

This division is made by the *Sept.,* though not by the other versions, nor the *Targ. Venema* adopts it. The word <span style="color:#008080">rwzm</span>, rendered above “cure,” means evidently a wound. It only occurs here, and Obadiah 7, and twice in Hosea 5:13, where it is rendered “wound.” It comes from <span style="color:#008080">rwz</span>, in the sense of compressing or binding up; but the noun taken passively, signifies what is compressed or bound up, and that is a wound. Then the literal rendering would be — <span style="color:#000080">None</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">pleading thy cause; As to the wound, medicatives, binding</span> <span style="color:#000080">up, none to thee;</span>

*or in other words, —* <span style="color:#000080">As to the wound, thou hast no medicatives, no binding up.</span>

The word <span style="color:#008080">twapr</span> means medicatives or medicaments, rather than medicines, as it designates here, and in most places, outward applications to wounds and bruises. The order, as in the foregoing verse, is reversed, the medicaments are put before the binding up. See Ezekiel 30:21. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF11</span> It is better to retain the literal word “lovers,” than “friends,” as rendered by the *Sept.,* the *Syr.,* and the *Targ.,* though not by the *Vulg.* The particle <span style="color:#008080">l[</span> is commonly a preposition, but not when preceding a verb, as here; and that <span style="color:#008080">br</span> is a verb here is proved by the sentence which follows, which is in apposition; and it is so rendered by *Blayney.* The verse may be thus rendered — <span style="color:#000080">14. All thy lovers have forgotten thee, Thee they seek not: Verily</span> <span style="color:#000080">with the stroke of an enemy have I struck thee, —  With a violent</span> <span style="color:#000080">correction; Because multiplied had thine iniquity, Grown strong</span> <span style="color:#000080">had thy sins, etc.</span>

The word for “violent,” or cruel, is so construed in the early versions;

<!-- PAGE 1297 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">the *Targ.* alone countenances our version. The last line conveys a different idea from the preceding. The verb, indeed, means strong in number as well as strong in power; but as number is expressed in the previous line, we may justly consider that power is meant here: their sins were not only many, but strong and vigorous, so strong as to resist all exhortations and all threatenings. — *Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF12</span> Rather “sore,” or wound. The word <span style="color:#008080">bakm</span> indeed means sometimes the soreness or wound of the mind, that is, sorrow or grief; but here, no doubt, it retains its primary idea, correspondently with stroke, bruise, and wound. The *Targ.* retains this meaning, while the versions go all astray. Then it is, “Miserable is thy sore.” The rest of the verse is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">Because multiplied had thine iniquity, Grown strong had thy sins,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Have I done these things to thee. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF13</span> What seems to be his meaning is, that as God had punished his people, *therefore* he would punish the nations. The versions and the *Targ.* render it “therefore;” but *Lowth* gives “yet surely;” and *Blayney,* “afterwards.” But we may render it “therefore,” or for this reason, as anticipative of what is contained at the end of the next verse, “Because an outcast have they called thee. Sion, whom no one seeks.” *Venema,* apprehending this to be the sense of the passage, supposed that the two verses have been transposed: but this kind of construction is not unfrequent in Scripture. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF14</span> The versions and the *Targ.* render the word for “palace,” temple; and as the former clause has “on its heap,” or, on its ruins, so in this the same preposition is used, and seems to require a similar construction, “on its former spot,” or, on its wonted place. The word <span style="color:#008080">fpçm</span> denotes what is customary or usual, as well as what is right and just. Then the two lines would read thus, — <span style="color:#000080">And built shall be the city on its ruins, And the palace on its</span> <span style="color:#000080">wonted seat shall be fixed, (or shall stand.)</span>

But the versions and the *Targ.* vary the meaning of the preposition. The *Vulg.,* with which the rest essentiall y agree, is, “And the temple according to its order, shall be founded.” *Blayney* renders the line thus, —

<!-- PAGE 1298 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And the palace shall be established upon its (former) plan.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">As in the previous line, the place is designated, it is probable that the place also is meant here. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF15</span> The meaning of the latter verbs in these clauses is to be ascertained by the preceding verbs: “I will multiply,” or increase “them;” then the opposite to this is, “and they shall not be lessened,” or decreased: and when it is said, “I will glorify,” or honor “them,” the corresponding contrast to this is, “they shall not be degraded,” or dishonored. The first clause refers to number, and the second to honor, dignity, or renown. Then the right version would be as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">And I will multiply them, and they shall not be lessened, I will also</span> <span style="color:#000080">honor them, and they shall not be degraded.</span> <span style="color:#000080">*— Ed.*</span> <span style="color:#800000">ftF16</span> It would be better to observe the order of the original, “And his assembling before me shall be confirmed,” or according, to the *Vulg.* and *Syr.,* “shall continue.” The reference is to the assembling at the stated festivals. The verb means to be confirmed, to be fixed, to be made certain; so that “continue” conveys the right idea: the assembling was to be made fixed, so as to become permanent; and it is said to be before God, in order to distinguish it from any other kind of assembling· — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF17</span> The *Vulg.* favors the meaning advocated by *Calvin,* “For who is this *(iste)* that will apply his heart to draw nigh to me, saith the Lord?” The *Sept.* is nearly the same, “For who is this (<span style="color:#0000d4">ou[tov</span>) who has given his heart to tuae to me, saith the Lord?” The *Syr.* is, “For I will turn his heart to me, saith the Lord.” The *Targ.* is as follows, “For who is this who will in his heart come to my worship, saith the Lord?”

Many explanations have been given which are wholly inadmissible, having nothing in the context to support them, such as the application of these words to our Savior. They are evidently connected with the previous clause, being joined with it by “for:” they in a manner explain and qualify that clause, and may be deemed parenthetic, for the former clause and that which follows these words, are connected together, — <span style="color:#000080">And I will bring him nigh that he may come near to me, (For who is</span> <span style="color:#000080">he who pledges his heart To come near to me, saith Jehovah!)</span>

<!-- PAGE 1299 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">22. And ye shall be to me a people, And I will be to you a God.</span></p>

By “him” we are to understand “Jacob,” the subject of the whole passage, and not the “governor,” who was to come from “the midst of him,” *i.e.,* Jacob, a name by which the whole nation is here called. The promise is to bring Jacob, or the people, nigh; and then to shew that this is alone God’s work, the words in the parenthesis are introduced, and by a question, which implies the negative in the strongest manner, as though he had said, “This work, to bring you nigh, is mine alone, for no one among you pledges or engages his heart to come near to me.”

Both the *Sept.* and the *Targ.* render “him” in the first line in the plural number, “them,” *i.e.,* the people. And the *Syr.,* though the form of the expression is changed, yet gives the *meaning* of the words within the parenthesis, for the work of turning the heart is ascribed to the Lord. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF18</span> There is a mistake as to this reference, for the word is only found in Psalm 73:122. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF19</span> This verse is literally the same, word for word, with the passage referred to here, with the exception of the word that is noticed; and yet neither the early versions nor the *Targum*  are the same: it is singular how they differ from themselves, so that we can have no confidence in their verbal accuracy. Instead of “the pregnant tempest” in Jeremiah 23:19, we have here “the violent tempest;” it is a participle from a verb which means to agitate, to excite, to stir up; and being passive, it means what is agitated or in great commotion, and hence violent or turbulent.

Many copies have <span style="color:#008080">w</span> here before <span style="color:#008080">r[s</span>, as in the former passage, where it is omitted only in one copy. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF20</span> The verse is literally as follows, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Turn not away shall the burning of Jehovah’s wrath, Until his</span> <span style="color:#000080">execution and until his completion Of the purposes of his heart: In</span> <span style="color:#000080">the latter days ye shall understand it.</span></p>

A verb in the infinitive mood in Hebrew is used often as a noun, “his execution.” A similar form exists in Welsh, *nes gwneuthur ohono.* “Until he hath confirmed,” or “performed,” according to our version, is better rendered in the *Vulg.,* “until he hath completed.” Here is the

<!-- PAGE 1300 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">execution and the completion. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF21</span> The early versions and the *Targum* vary much as to the meaning of this and the following verse. The nearest to the Original, as a whole, is the *Vulg.;* the *Sept.* go wholly astray. Of all the expositions which have been given, that of *Calvin* seems the best, as it corresponds more with the Hebrew. I render the second verse thus, — <span style="color:#000080">Thus saith Jehovah, —  Find favor in the wilderness Did the</span> <span style="color:#000080">people, the remnants of the sword, When proceeding to his rest</span> <span style="color:#000080">*was*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Israel.</span>

I take <span style="color:#008080">˚wlh</span> as a participle, the auxiliary verb being understood, as the case often is in Hebrew. Preceded by a preposition, and followed by a pronoun, <span style="color:#008080">[ygrh</span> is a verb in the infinitive mood, used as a noun. Twelve MSS., says *Blayney,* have <span style="color:#008080">˚lh</span> a past tense in *Kal:* if so, then the meaning would be more striking, though somewhat elliptical, — <span style="color:#000080">Proceed (or advance) to his rest did Israel.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">As though he had said, “The people, who escaped the sword of Pharaoh and the slaughters which happened to them, found favor during their passage through the wilderness, and notwithstanding all opposition, Israel advanced forward to his promised rest.” — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF22</span> I find nothing satisfactory as to this verse, except the explanation here given, and it is that of the *Targum.* The first clause is the people’s cavilling answer to what is declared in the foregoing verse. Jacob is the person introduced, as representing the people. He says, it is indeed true, — <span style="color:#000080">“At a remote period Jehovah appeared to me.”</span>

Then the rejoinder to this is exactly suitable, — <span style="color:#000080">But with perpetual love have I loved thee,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Therefore have I prolonged to thee mercy.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Or, “extended to thee mercy,” (see <span style="color:#800000"><19A912></span>Psalm 109:12,) or, “continued to thee mercy,” or, according to *Blayney,* “lengthened out mercy to thee.” Now there is a consistency in the whole passage, according to this view, and also in what follows, “I will again build thee,” etc. *— Ed.*</p>

<!-- PAGE 1301 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftF23</span> See Exodus 15:20; Judges 11:34; 1 Samuel 18:6; Psalm 68:25. “Tabrets” or timbrels were carried in one hand, and beaten by the fingers of the other. It was a hoop with bits of brass, and over this hoop parchment was distended.; they were very like what are now called tambourins. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF24</span> This verb seems not to have been rightly understood by the authors of the early versions, nor by the writer of the *Targum.* Their imperfect knowledge of Hebrew frequently appears. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF25</span> The verb for “cry” is either in the past tense or in the imperative mood. As there are so many imperatives in this passage, it seems that this is an imperative too. It appears that the latter part of the last verse, this verse, and that which follows, contain what would be addressed to the people after their return. In no other way can the verbs be grammatically rendered. The whole address is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">“Plant, O ye planters, and eat the fruit;</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">6. For come is the day: Call ye, O</span> <span style="color:#000080">watchmen, on mount Ephraim,</span> <span style="color:#000080">‘Arise, and let us go into Sion, to Jehovah our God;’</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">7. For thus has Jehovah said, Shout ye, ‘To Jacob</span> <span style="color:#000080">*there is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">joy,’</span> <span style="color:#000080">And cry it aloud amont the chief of the nations; Publish, exultingly</span> <span style="color:#000080">proclaim, and say, ‘Saved hath Jehovah thy people, The remnant</span> <span style="color:#000080">of Israel.’”</span></p>

The passage is a sort of an episode. What follows seems well connected with the former part of the 5th verse (Jeremiah 31:5).

“Eat the fruit,” is the meaning, and not the literal version, which can hardly be given: it is so rendered by *Blayney.* “Call ye,” or, proclaim, or, give the invitation. The news was to be made known “among the chief of the nations,” as it is evident from the 10th verse (Jeremiah 31:10). “Saved,” etc., so the Sept. and the *Targum,* and more consistent with the context than “save;” but both have “his,” *i.e.,* God’s, instead of “thy people,” *i.e.,* Jacob’s. The verb <span style="color:#008080">wllh</span> means not only to praise, but also to boast, to exult, and here evidently to proclaim with exultation or triumph. It is rendered here “sing ye,” by the *Vulg.* and *Syr.*

<!-- PAGE 1302 -->
It is worthy of notice, that in this episode the particulars, mentioned in the 4th verse (Jeremiah 31:4-5), and the beginning of the 5th, are referred to in their reversed order. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF26</span> The *Targ.* and the Versions, excepting the *Vulg.,* give a similar meaning to these two clauses. They give the sense of “departing” to the first verb, while it commonly has the sense of “coming.” It is also in the future tense, and therefore cannot refer to the departing of the Israelites, who are meant here, for they had already gone into exile. Their return is no doubt what is spoken of, which would be attended with “weeping,” not for joy, but for their sins, as it is distinctly expressed in verses 18 and 19 (Jeremiah 31:18-19); and also with entreaties or supplications. And it is better with *Venema* to join the two words with “coming,” — <span style="color:#000080">With weeping shall they come and with supplications; I will bring</span> <span style="color:#000080">them, I will lead them, By streams of water, in a straight way;</span> <span style="color:#000080">They shall not stumble in it.</span>

He promises two things, to “bring” and to “lead;” then the leading refers to the streams of water, and the bringing to the straight way; which is a kind of arrangement that is often to be met with in Scripture. Two things, especially necessary for travelers, are promised, water and a good road. “Straight” seems to apply to the surface of the road as well as to its sides; hence some render it “smooth” or even, such as would have nothing that might cause one to stumble. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF27</span> What is here said is no doubt true: but the auxiliary verb is, “I was,” not “I shall be;” and so it is rendered by the *Sept., Vulg.,* and *Targ.;* and by the *Syr.,* I am. Then the Versions, very incorrectly, give the next clause, in which there is no verb, in the present tense, while it ought to be in the past tense, like the foregoing. The words literally are, — <span style="color:#000080">For I was (or, have been) to Jacob a Father, And Ephraim, my</span> <span style="color:#000080">first-born he</span> <span style="color:#000080">*(i.e. was*</span> <span style="color:#000080">he.)</span>

<!-- PAGE 1303 -->
And to this purpose has *Blayney* rendered the passage. Whenever the auxiliary verb is understood, its tense must be regulated by the context. On “first-born,” see Exodus 4:22, 23, and 1 Chronicles 5:1. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF28</span> The difference between the two verbs seems to be this:, <span style="color:#008080">hdp</span> is to rescue, to free, to deliver, either by force or by a ransom; but <span style="color:#008080">lag</span> is to recover what one has a right to, and this also either by force or by a ransom. So that the latter implies a claim or right which is not intimated by the former, — <span style="color:#000080">For Jehovah will deliver Jacob,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And recover him from a hand stronger than his own.</span>

Forcible deliverance is no doubt meant here; and the latter verb is very striking, as it implies that God was vindicating his own right in extricating Jacob from the grasp of a hand stronger than his own. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF29</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">rhg</span> rendered here, “flow together,” has another meaning, “to be enlightened” or illuminated, (see Psalm 34:5; ) and light in Scripture means comfort, delight, or enjoyment. It is so taken by the *Syriac* and the *Targ.,* and more suitably to the words which follow than in the sense here given, — <span style="color:#000080">And they shall be comforted by the bounty of Jehovah, With corn,</span> <span style="color:#000080">and with new wine, and with oil, Also with the young of the flock</span> <span style="color:#000080">and of the herd; And their soul shall be like a watered garden, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">they shall again hunger no more.</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">*Or,* <span style="color:#000080">And they shall again feel want no more.</span> <span style="color:#000080">*— Ed.*</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF30</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">bad</span>, here used, does not mean to mourn or to “sorrow,” though this is the idea given to it by the *Targ.* It is rendered “hunger,” by the *Sept.* and *Vulg.* According to *Parkhurst,* its real meaning is, “to faint or fail through weariness, hunger, or terror.” *Blayney*  renders, “pine for hunger.” See the previous note. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF31</span> This clause may be rendered thus, — .

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">For I will turn their mourning into rejoicing, And I will comfort</span> <span style="color:#000080">them and cheer them above their sorrow.</span></p>

That is, “I will give them comfort and joy more than the sorrow which they have had.” The preposition <span style="color:#008080">m</span>, has often the meaning of *above* or *more than.* See Deuteronomy 14:2. Their sorrow had been great, but the promise here is, to give them in proportion a comfort and a joy

<!-- PAGE 1304 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">still greater. — *Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF32</span> “To be not,” according to the usage of the Scripture, means either dead or absent. See Genesis 42:36. Joseph was not, he being dead; and Simeon was not, he being absent in Egypt. To be not here refers to the absent, those driven into exile; but the passage, as quoted by Matthew, refers to such as were dead. The similarity was only in part, that is, as to the weeping. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF33</span> The quotation in Matthew is neither from the Hebrew nor from the *Sept.* It is substantially correct, but not verbally; the sense and not the words, seems to have been chiefly regarded by the Apostles. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF34</span> “Ramah” is found in the *Sept.,* the *Syr.,* and the *Targ.;* but “on the height,” or, on high, is the *Vulg*. It seems better to retain the proper name, “Ramah.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF35</span> Or the words may be rendered, “There is a hope for thy posterity.” So *Gataker,* not without reason, renders the words. The following clause explains what this *“hope”* was. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF36</span> The idea of “transmigrating” is alone given by the *Vulg.,* the other versions and the *Targ.* have “lamenting:” and the latter is more consonant with the context, and has been adopted by almost all modern commentators. It is used in Jeremiah 15:5, in the sense of being moved or affected for another, of sympathizing or condoling. It is there in its simple form, that is, in *Kal.* As it is here in *Hithpael,* its meaning is, self-condoling, or condoling himself, — an idea which is very expressive, and is more fully explained in the next verse. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF37</span> This is no doubt the right rendering, and not, “Thou art Jehovah my God.” So in the first commandment, the version ought to be, “I Jehovah,” or, I the Lord, “am thy God.” The meaning is not, that he is Jehovah, but that he who is Jehovah is our God. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1305 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftF38</span> The *Vulg.* and the *Targ.* favor this view of a different sense of the same verb in the second clause. The *Sept.* retain the same meaning. There is no need of altering the sense; indeed, another sense does not so well comport with the passage. He says that God had chastised him, and that he was chastised as an untamed, or rather untrained steer or bullock, implying that he was compelled to bear the yoke, and also that he had been brought to submit to it: hence the prayer that follows, “turn,” or rather, restore, etc. The verb <span style="color:#008080">rsy</span> means to correct rather than to chastise, even to correct by the rod, or by the goad; and then to teach as the effect of correction, — <span style="color:#000080">Thou hast corrected me; Yea, I was corrected like a steer, not</span> <span style="color:#000080">trained:  Restore thou me, and I shall be restored;  For thou,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah, art my God.</span>

After a confession with regard to correction, a confession that intimates that it had its proper effect, a prayer for restoration seems suitable, and that prayer is founded on the fact that Jehovah was their God. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF39</span> What *Calvin* teaches here is indisputable, but whether the passage warrants the view he takes of it, is another thing, though most commentators have taken the same view. The versions, especially the *Vulg.,* seem to have suggested this explanation by giving to the verb <span style="color:#008080">bwç</span>, in the former verse, the meaning of turning or conversion, instead of returning or restoring, agreeably with the whole context, see verse 17th (Jeremiah 31:17). *Gataker* suggested this idea; and it was afterwards fully adopted by *Venema:* and, according to their views I render this verse as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">For after I returned to myself, I repented, And after I knew myself,</span> <span style="color:#000080">I smote my thigh;  I was ashamed and even confounded, Because I</span> <span style="color:#000080">have borne the reproach of my youth.</span>

The *Vulg.* renders the first words, “After thou hast turned,” or converted “me (convertisti me; )” the *Sept.,* “After my captivity;” the *Syr.,* “After that I was converted;” and the *Targ.,* “When we return to the Law.” Literally the words are, “After my returning,” which, according to the Hebrew idiom may be rendered, “After returning to myself,” as in the following line, “after my knowing,” means evidently “after knowing myself.”

The two verses contain the language of the penitent, praying for restoration to their own land: and two reasons are assigned for this prayer, — because Jehovah was their covenanted God, — and because they repented, for to such had restoration been promised: Hence for is used twice; it is therefore not right to render <span style="color:#008080">yk</span> at the beginning of the 19th verse (Jeremiah 31:19), verily or surely. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1306 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftF40</span> This verse has been variously explained. The two questions are taken by *Calvin* and by others as strong negatives: but this is not always the ease; both <span style="color:#008080">h</span> and <span style="color:#008080">µa</span> are often taken as strong affirmatives. See Jeremiah 3:6; 1 Samuel 2:27; Ezekiel 20:30; Amos 6:2. This sense is what the context requires; for this verse is an answer to penitent Ephraim. Neither the *Sept.,* nor the *Vulg.,* nor the *Syr.,* nor the *Targ.* retain the interrogatory form: but they retain the meaning, if the questions be taken affirmatively, not otherwise. The next words I render thus, — <span style="color:#000080">For since my words</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">in him,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Remembering I will still remember him.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">This is according to the *Sept.,* and the general drift of the *Targ.* The *Syr.* gives another meaning, — <span style="color:#000080">For at the time when I speak against him,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Remembering I still remember him.</span></p>

There are no other versions which come so near to the original. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF41</span> The word for “sounded,” means to tumultuate, to be agitated, to be greatly moved or disturbed. It is rendered by the *Vulg.,* “are troubled — conturbata;” by the *Syr.* and *Targ.,* “are moved.” It may be rendered “trouble” here. See Isaiah 16:1 l, where the action of the bowels is compared to the harp, not surely to its sound, but to the vibration of its cords. See also Isaiah 63:15, and Cant. v. 4. *— Ed.*

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#800000">ftF42</span> <span style="color:#000080">Raise up for thyself heaps, Fix for thyself pillars.</span></p>

Instead of <span style="color:#008080">µynyx</span> many copies read <span style="color:#008080">µynwyx</span> see 2 Kings 23:17; Ezekiel 39:15. That <span style="color:#008080">µyrwrmt</span> cannot mean “bitternesses,” as rendered by the *Vulg.* and the *Targ.,* is evident from the verb that precedes it, which means to place, to set, to fix. <span style="color:#008080">rmt</span> is the palm-tree; the word then means palm-tree pillars, or pillars straight and high as the palm-tree. The remainder of the verse is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">Set thy heart on the highway, The way thou didst go;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Return, O virgin of Israel, return, To thy cities ascend.</span>

The word for “highway” means a raised road, a road prepared for travelling. The “virgin” here signifies one that is of an age to be married. When Israel repented, they were in a fit state to be united to God, as

<!-- PAGE 1307 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">their husband. The last line is rendered according to the proposed emendation of *Houbigant* and approved by *Horsley,* <span style="color:#008080">hl[</span> instead of, <span style="color:#008080">hla</span> which seems to have no meaning. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF43</span> The verb, rendered “going about,” only occurs here in *Hithpael,* and once in *Kal,* Cant. v. 6; where it means to “withdraw,” or recede, or turn aside. And this sense of withdrawing is what is given to it here both by the *Sept.,* the *Targ.,* and the *Syr.;* but it is the withdrawing from accepting the return offered. We may give this version, —

How long wilt thou decline, (i.e., to return,) O daughter of the restoration? She had been before exhorted to return in the previous verse: she is now blamed for her unwillingness, which seems to have arisen from fear, and a sense of weakness. Then comes in most appropriately what follows, if interpreted according to the explanation of *Calvin.* The verb <span style="color:#008080">bç</span>, the root of, <span style="color:#008080">hbbwçh</span>, means more frequently to turn to, to return, than to turn away, to apostatize. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF44</span> Whatever may be the meaning of this clause, it cannot certainly be applied to the miraculous conception of our Saviour, and for this plain reason, as *Blayney* observes, that the only thing the passage announces, if viewed in this light, is this, — that a woman shall conceive a male child, which is nothing new, but a common event; for the word here for “woman,” is not what signifies a virgin, but what designates only the sex; it means properly a female as distinguished from a male. *Henry,* as well as *Blayney* and Adam *Clarke,* agree materially with *Calvin,* as to the meaning of this sentence. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF45</span> The principal objection to this interpretation has been, that it was not by overcoming their enemies by force of arms that the Jews returned. The answer to this is, that this is a sort of proverbial expression, intimating that the weakest would prevail over the strongest. Besides, though the Jews returned by virtue of the edict of the king of Persia, yet they had many and strong enemies to oppose them. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF46</span> *Blaney*  renders the verse, thus, — .

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And Judah shall dwell in it and all his cities, Husbandmen together,</span> <span style="color:#000080">and they shall go about with flocks.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1308 -->
Like *Calvin* he takes “cities” for citizens; but still there is an inappropriateness in the words. I regard the word “land” as understood before “Judah,” — <span style="color:#000080">And dwell in it,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*the land*</span> <span style="color:#000080">of Judah, And in all his cities together,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Shall husbandmen; and they shall remove with</span> <span style="color:#000080">*their*</span> <span style="color:#000080">flocks.</span>

See Jeremiah 33:12,13,where the meaning of what is here said is made more evident “Remove,” that is, from place to place, as the word means, for the purpose of feeding their flocks. This betokened a state of liberty and of security. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF47</span> Both the *Sept.* and Syr. render the first word “thirsty,” and the second, “hungry,” agreeably with the verbs which precede them. The “weary” and “sorrowful” of our version are no doubt wrong; the first, adopted from the *Vulg.;* and the second from the *Targ.* <span style="color:#000080">For I will water the thirsty soul, And every hungry soul will I fill.</span>

“Soul” here means the person, the individual, — “ I will water him that is thirsty,” etc. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF48</span> It is difficult to see exactly the purport of this verse: what does “beheld,” or saw mean? May not the verb be considered as in *Hophal,* “I was made to see,” or, had a vision? The verb is often used in this sense: see Zechariah 3:1. Then we may give this version, — <span style="color:#000080">Hereupon I awoke and had a vision;</span> <span style="color:#000080">And my sleep had been pleasant to me.</span>

Visions were of two kinds, given in sleep, and given to persons awake: the previous vision was given to the Prophet while he was asleep. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF49</span> I am disposed to render the latter part of this verse according to the *Syriac, —* <span style="color:#000080">That I will sow, as to the house of Israel and the house of Judah,</span> <span style="color:#000080">The seed of man and the seed of beast.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1309 -->
I take <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> as a preposition, which it often is. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF50</span> The words here used are the very same with those in Jeremiah 1:10, except the addition, “to afflict;” and yet neither the *Targ.,* nor the Versions, except the *Syriac,* render them alike, giving in some instances the meaning of one verb to another, — a proof that they are very loose versions. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF51</span> *Carm., Lib.* 3, *Od.* 6.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF52</span> The *Targum* thus interprets this proverb, “The fathers have sinned, and the children have been smitten.” “Blunted,” or deprived of feeling, *obstupuerunt,* is both the *Vulg.* and the *Syr. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF53</span> This clause, as quoted in Hebrews 8:9, is, “And I regarded them not,” according to the *Sept.* and the *Syr.,.* though the *Vulg.* is the same with our version. *Houbigant* says, “Read <span style="color:#008080">ytl[g</span> I have rejected, I have repudiated.” The only difference is in one letter; and this word is used by Jeremiah in Jeremiah 14:19. There would thus be a perfect correspondence, “and I rejected them, saith Jehovah.”

“Which my covenant,” in the previous clause, is the *Vulg.;* but according to the *Sept.,* the *Syr.,* and the *Targ.,* it is, “because they have broken my covenant,” etc. <span style="color:#008080">rça</span> is not used, as given in our version, in connection with a noun that follows, though it is so used with pronouns. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF54</span> All the nouns in Hebrew are of the singular number, — “law, inward part, heart,” and also “iniquity and sin;” and so are they in the *Vulg.,* except the second, which is rendered “bowels;” but in the other versions and the *Targ.,* they are mostly pluralized. The words as quoted in Hebrews are not exactly according to any of the versions, but for the most part according to that of the *Sept.*

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">There is in many copies a <span style="color:#008080">w</span> before <span style="color:#008080">yttn</span>, “I have put,” by which it is turned into a future, “I will even put.” This seems to be the true reading, — <span style="color:#000080">I will even put my law in their inmost part,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And on their heart will I write it.</span></p>

It is the same as if it was said, “I will put my law in the inmost part of each of them:” the persons are individualized, in order to shew that the act extends to every one alike. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF55</span> Literally the words are, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And they shall teach no more, a man his neighbor, And a man his</span> <span style="color:#000080">brother, by saying, “Know ye Jehovah;” For all of them shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">know me, From the least of them to the greatest of them, Saith</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1310 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftF56</span> “Ordinance,” and *“ordinances,”* would perhaps be the best words. The word means a fixed order of things, sometimes rendered in our version”statute,” and sometimes “ordinance,” — <span style="color:#000080">Thus saith Jehovah, —  He who hath appointed the sun for light</span> <span style="color:#000080">by day, The ordinance of the moon and stars for light by night,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Who calms the sea when roar do its waves, —  Jehovah of hosts is</span> <span style="color:#000080">his name,</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Two opposite meanings are given by many Lexicons to <span style="color:#008080">[gr</span> — to divide, to break, to cleave, and also to give rest, to calm. *Parkhurst* holds that it has only the last. However, that it means here to make quiet, or to calm, is most probable, as God’s restraining power as to the sea is mentioned before in Jeremiah 5:22 as a proof of his greatness.</p>

The word for *“ordinance”* before *“moon”* is left out in the *Sept.,* and in one MS.; and the passage would read better without it*. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF57</span> The whole of this passage is differently rendered in the early versions and the *Targum;* some of them evidently wrong and some doubtful. *Blayney* gives the most literal and most consistent version. I give the following, — <span style="color:#000080">38. Behold the days are coming, saith Jehovah, That built shall the</span> <span style="color:#000080">city be for (or to) Jehovah, From the tower of Hananeel to the gate</span> <span style="color:#000080">of the corner:</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">39. Yea, go forth again shall the measuring line  From over against</span> <span style="color:#000080">it, over the hill of Oareb,  And shall surround Goath</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">40.And all the valley of the carcases and ashes, And all the fields to</span> <span style="color:#000080">the river Kidron, To the corner of the gate of the horses eastward:</span> <span style="color:#000080">Holy to Jehovah, it shall not be rooted up,  Nor demolished any</span> <span style="color:#000080">more for ever.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1311 -->
The 38th verse (Jeremiah 31:38) contains a general description; this is particularized in the following verses. The beginning of measuring was to be at “the tower of Hananeel;” hence “from over against it,” or before it: the “gate” being feminine cannot be meant; it is then “the tower.” As to the word for “fields,” the reading of the *Keri* and of several MSS., countenanced by the *Vulg.,* ought no doubt to be adopted. “Eastward,” — thus the line came round to the same point where it began; for the tower of Hananeel was eastward. But what is referred to in the two last lines? The verbs are in the masculine gender, and “city” is feminine; and there is nothing in the passage with which they can agree except the tower of Hananeel. Then this tower seems to stand here for the rebuilt city; and then rooting up, *i.e.,* undermining the foundations, and demolishing, are suitably applied to a tower. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF58</span> Some think, such as *Gataker* and *Blayney,* that according to the description here given, the dimensions of the city are much larger than what they had ever been before. The “line” was to inclose a part at least of the hill of Gareb, the whole of Goath, supposed to be Golgotha, the valley of the carcases, and the fields of Kidron, all which were formerly without the walls of the city. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF59</span> The 2d, 3d, 4th, and 5th verses (Jeremiah 32:1-5) ought to be put as parenthetical, as they only relate the circumstances connected with Jeremiah when he received the vision which he proceeds to relate in the 6th verse. Instead of “For then” in the 2d verse, “And then” would be more proper; and “Where” would be better than “For” at the beginning of the 3d verse, “Where Zedekiah had shut him up,” etc. It is “In which” in the *Sept. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF60</span> *Gataker* and *Venema* give another view of this clause. The Lord, as we find from verse 7 (Jeremiah 32:7), did not tell him to *buy* the field, but only informed him of the coming and offer of Hanameel. When Hanameel came, he knew that it was God’s will that he should buy the field, and he instantly acted accordingly. He knew from the very circumstances that it was God’s message, sent to him to buy the field. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF61</span> We may render the words literally thus, “And I weighed for him the money, seven shekels and ten, the money.” The word is “silver,” but it is often taken for money. The seventeen shekels, according to *Lowth,* were about two pounds of our money. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1312 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftF62</span> There were no doubt two rolls or writings, as it appears clear from Jeremiah 32:14, where the two are distinctly mentioned, “Take these rolls, this roll of the purchase, even the sealed, and this open roll,” etc. The word <span style="color:#008080">rps</span> ought to be rendered throughout either a roll, or a book, or writing, when preceded by an article, and not “evidence,” as in our version.

There seems to be an incongruity in verse the 10th (Jeremiah 32:10), as rendered by most; the roll is represented as “sealed,” before the “witnesses” are mentioned, and before the money was weighed. The rendering, I conceive, ought to be as follows, “So I wrote in a roll; and I sealed it, when I had made witnesses to witness *it,* and weighed the money in balances.” The <span style="color:#008080">w</span> may often be rendered “when,” and also “so,” as it is done in the next verse in our version, though there “and” would be more suitable. The “witnesses” are expressly said in ver. 12 (Jeremiah 32:12) to have subscribed, or written in, the book or roll of purchase, which was sealed. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF63</span> The 11th verse (Jeremiah 32:11), where the two rolls are first mentioned, is difficult to be rendered. I offer the following literal version of the 11th, 12th, 13th and 14th, — <span style="color:#000080">11. And I took the writing of the purchase, — it the sealed, the</span> <span style="color:#000080">commandment and the conditions, — and it the open; and I gave</span> <span style="color:#000080">the writing of the purchase to Baruch the son of Neriah,</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">12 The son of Maaseiah, in the sight of Hanameel my uncle’s son,</span> <span style="color:#000080">and in the sight of the witnesses who wrote in the roll of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">purchase, and in sight of all the Jews who</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">13. sat in the court of the prison; and I commanded Baruch in their</span> <span style="color:#000080">sight,</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">14. saying, “Thus saith Jehovah of hosts, the God of Israel, ‘Take</span> <span style="color:#000080">these rolls, — this roll of the purchase, even it the sealed, and this</span> <span style="color:#000080">open roll; and put them in au earthen vessel, that they may</span> <span style="color:#000080">continue many days.’”</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The two rolls are called “the writing of the purchase” in ver. 11, but distinguished, one being “sealed,” and the other “open,” or unsealed. The sealed contained “the commandment,” that is, to purchase, and “the conditions,” literally “ordinances;” which *Blayney*  renders, “the assignment and the limitations,” and the *Vulg.,* “the stipulations and ratifications.” But <span style="color:#008080">hwxm</span> never means anything but a precept or a commandment, and <span style="color:#008080">µyqj</span> are things  defined, settled, or appointed,</p>

<!-- PAGE 1313 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">and may be rendered “stipulations.” Thus the contents of the sealed roll are specified, but those of the open roll are not expressly mentioned. *— Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF64</span> The *Targ.* and the versions, except the *Vulg.,* give the first sense; but the latter is no doubt the true meaning, as the word never means properly to be hidden. The phrase here literally is, “Not harder (or more marvellous) than thou shall anything be,”that is, not harder than what thou canst do. Exactly the same phrase occurs in Genesis 18:14. The word, in a similar clause, in Deuteronomy 30:11, is rendered “hidden;” but the clause literally is, “It is not harder than thou,” that is, than what thou canst attain, or do, as the context proves, see Jeremiah 32:14. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF65</span> The change of person seems to begin at the 18th verse (Jeremiah 32:18), and includes the first clause in the 19th, — <span style="color:#000080">18. He who sheweth mercy to thousands, And who returns the</span> <span style="color:#000080">iniquity of fathers To the bosom of their children after them,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">God, the great, the powerful; Jehovah is his name, —</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">19. Great in counsel and mighty in his doings: Who — thine eyes</span> <span style="color:#000080">are open On the ways of the sons of men, To give to each</span> <span style="color:#000080">according to his ways, And according to the fruit of his doings;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">20. Who, etc., etc.</span></p>

“God, the great,” etc., is connected with shewing mercy and requiting iniquity. His greatness is in counsel or wisdom, and his power or might is manifested in his doings. The <span style="color:#008080">h</span> after doings is the Chaldee for <span style="color:#008080">w</span> Then his omniscience is referred to, as necessary for carrying to effect his purposes and directing his doings. Here he returns to the second person, and the “who” is idiomatic, and the *Welsh* is exactly the same, *Yr hwn y mae dy lygaid,* etc.; and the “Who” is continued in the Jeremiah 32:20. In saying that each is to have “according to his ways,” he intimates what *Calvin* says, that the children like the fathers are guilty. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF66</span> This is commonly the meaning given to this verse. It may be rendered as follows, —

<!-- PAGE 1314 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">20. Who hast set signs and wonders in the land of Egypt,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*To before*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*signs and wonders*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to this day  Both to Israel and to mankind;  And</span> <span style="color:#000080">hast made to thyself a name,  Such as it is at this day.</span></p>

They were “signs” or evidences of God’s power, and in their character “wonders,” that is, supernatural. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF67</span> There is this difference between these three things: the “voice” was that of God by his prophets, — the “law” was the ten commandments, — and “all which” had been “commanded” were the statutes and ordinances, the civil and ceremonial appointments. To “hearken to his voice,” rather than to obey it, is what is meant: so far from obeying it, they would not hear the Prophets. This had been throughout their sin. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF68</span> The particle <span style="color:#008080">˚a</span> means also truly, verily, surely. It is rendered “only,” by the *Sept., —* “continually,” by the *Vulg., —* “verily,” by the *Targ.,* — and is omitted by the *Syr;*  Its most proper meaning is, “nevertheless,” but is often rendered “surely” in our version: and it might be rendered here, surely or doubtless. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF69</span> It appears evident, that the last clause of this is explanatory of the first, as *Calvin* shews. “The evil,” for the article precedes it, was “the work of their hands,” that is, idolatry: <span style="color:#000080">For the children of Israel and the children of Judah have doubtless</span> <span style="color:#000080">been doing the evil before mine eyes</span> <span style="color:#000080">from their youth; for the</span> <span style="color:#000080">children of Israel have doubtless been provoking me with the work</span> <span style="color:#000080">of their hands, saith Jehovah.</span>

The connection in this way appears more obvious. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF70</span> It has been found difficult to render this verse literally, though the general meaning is evident, and is given in our version, which is more paraphrastic than usual. If we take <span style="color:#008080">l[</span> for <span style="color:#008080">hl[</span>, in its Chaldee sense, as in Daniel 6:5, 6, we shall find the version easy, — <span style="color:#000080">31. For the occasion of my wrath, and the occasion of my</span> <span style="color:#000080">indignation, has this city been to me from the day that they have</span> <span style="color:#000080">built it even</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">32. to this day; so that I shall remove it from my sight on account</span> <span style="color:#000080">of all the wickedness of the children of Israel, etc. etc.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1315 -->

So the latter part of Jeremiah 32:31 ought to be connected with the following verse. The verb for “remove” is in the infinitive mood preceded by <span style="color:#008080">l</span>. It is an elliptical phrase, as is sometimes the case, where a resolution, obligation, or duty is intended. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF71</span> So the original is; but we say the back. The same words are found in Jeremiah 2:27. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF72</span> The words for teaching, and early rising, are participles, dependent on “me,” in the previous clause, and by making a little change in the order of the words, the sense would be more evident, — <span style="color:#000080">And they turned the neck and not the face to me, while teaching</span> <span style="color:#000080">them, early-rising and teaching; yet they hearkened not to receive</span> <span style="color:#000080">instruction.</span>

They turned their back, while God was teaching them! — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF73</span> It is true that the word means correction as well as instruction; but as “teaching” is what was previously mentioned, our version, which gives the latter word, seems to present the true meaning here. It is so rendered by *Blayney. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF74</span> The *Vulg.* and the *Targ.* very incorrectly render the words, “In which my name is called.” The *Sept.* and the *Syr.* are the same as our version. It was, no doubt, a house of prayer; but what is here meant is, that it was called God’s house. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1316 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftF75</span> In Jeremiah 7:31, we have “the high places,” or elevations, “of Tophet.” *Blayney* thinks that they were artificial mounts thrown up for the purpose of performing some of their superstitious rites. Trees were, no doubt, planted on some of the high places; but there might be mounts without trees. That these high places were in a valley, favor the idea that they were artificial mounts without trees. And it indeed appears from this verse and from Jeremiah 7:31, that the image of Molech was set on the artificial mounts, for it is said that they *built* or erected these high places for this purpose, — that they might *burn* their children to Molech. And, probably, there were several mounts in this valley, in order to accommodate a large number of people. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF76</span> There is no ground for this supposition as to the practice in Tophet; for, in other parts of Scripture, what they did is specifically mentioned. In this very book it is said, that they *burnt* their children *in* *the fire,* Jeremiah 7:31, and that they *burnt* them as *burnt- offerings* to Baal, Jeremiah 19:5. See also Deuteronomy 12:31; Ezekiel 23:37. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF77</span> The *Keri,* <span style="color:#008080">ayfjh</span> “to cause to sin,” is no doubt the true reading, even the <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> before Judah is a proof of it, and it is the meaning given by the versions and the *Targ. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF78</span> “Ye *are* saying,” is the original, which betokens a habit at that time; they were continually saying this during the siege. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF79</span> This promise clearly shews what *Calvin* says as to their meaning in saying “The city has been delivered up,” etc. that is, irretrievably. No, says God, I will restore it. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF80</span> There is a gradation in the words, — wrath, hot wrath, foaming wrath. Extreme displeasure betokens, as *Calvin*  intimates, extreme wickedness, and inflicts extreme punishment. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF81</span> “One heart” and “one way,” seem to refer to the previous divisions between Israel and Judah. They were before divided in thoughts respecting God. and in the way of worshipping him. This division would no longer exist. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF82</span> The <span style="color:#008080">rça</span> may be rendered *that,* or *because. It* would be a perpetual covenant, because he would “not turn from *being* after them to do them good,” or, as the *Syr.* is, “from following them to do them good.” The *Vulg.* omits <span style="color:#008080">rça</span> and so does the *Targ.;* the *Syr.* gives it the meaning of *that,* but it is rendered *which,* by the *Sept.,* “which (that is covenant) I will not turn away from behind them,” that is, as it seems, from those behind them, *i.e.,* posterity. And this is the meaning which *Blayney* has adopted, “which I will not withdraw from their posterity, to be a benefactor to them;” which last words he evidently connects with the first clause. What favors this rendering is, that “children” are mentioned in the previous verse.

Still, owing to the last clause, the *Syriac* version seems to be the most suitable. There are here two remarkable promises, — that God would not turn away from them, — and that he would put in his fear, so as to keep them from turning away from him. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1317 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftF83</span> The word <span style="color:#008080">tma</span> most commonly means *reality* in opposition to dissimulation or pretense, or what is only apparent; *truth* in opposition to falsehood, and *stability* in opposition to what is evanescent and temporary. The planting was to be a *real* planting, and not one in disguise or appearance. The following words explain the meaning, “with all my heart and with all my soul,” that is, with sincerity and earnestness, or, with the full purpose of mind and with the full assent of the will and affections. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF84</span> This is not the literal rendering of the Hebrew, but the following, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Which, ye say,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">desolate, without man or beast, Given into the</span> <span style="color:#000080">hand of the Chaldeans.</span></p>

Had “which” been governed by “say,” there would have been a pronoun after it with a preposition prefixed. The *Sept.,* the *Syr.,* and *Arab.* have retained the right construction, though the *Vulg.* has not. “Without,” *i.e.,* with not, or not with, is literally the Hebrew, <span style="color:#008080">ˆyam</span> — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF85</span> The infinitives in Hebrew are often as in *Welsh,* verbal nouns. The rendering may be made as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">Fields with money shall they buy; And</span> <span style="color:#000080">*there shall be*</span> <span style="color:#000080">writing in a</span> <span style="color:#000080">book, And sealing, and the witnessing of witnesses, In the land of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Benjamin, etc. etc.</span>

Our version is the *Syr.* and nearly the *Targ. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF86</span> The *Sept.* give the present time, “who makes,” etc.; the *Vulg.* the future, “who will make,” etc.; and the *Syr.* and the *Targ.* in the past, “who made,” etc. The verse may be thus rendered, — <span style="color:#000080">Thus saith Jehovah, —  Made it hath Jehovah, Having formed it in</span> <span style="color:#000080">order to establish it;  Jehovah</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">his name.</span>

That the city is meant cannot be disputed, as the word itself is introduced in the 4th verse (Jeremiah 33:4), and at the end of the 5th verse. In the *Sept.* it is land, “who makes the land,” and in the *Syr.,* “who made thee:” both which are no doubt wrong. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF87</span> These two verses have been improperly separated, so that <span style="color:#008080">µyab</span>, “coming,” stands by itself without connection with anything; it ought to be *in regimine* with “sword.” The versions vary, but none give any tolerable meaning. The verses may be thus rendered, —

<!-- PAGE 1318 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">4. For thus saith Jehovah, the God of Israel, Concerning the houses</span> <span style="color:#000080">of this city, And concerning the houses of the kings of Judah, —</span> <span style="color:#000080">Which are thrown down by the engines,</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">5. And by the sword of those who come To make war, even the</span> <span style="color:#000080">Chaldeans, And to fill them [</span><span style="color:#000080">*i.e.,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">ηουσεσ with the carcases of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">men,  Whom I have smitten in mine anger, And in my wrath, and</span> <span style="color:#000080">for all whose wickedness I have hidden my face from this city, —</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">6. Behold, I will bring, etc. etc.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The present and past time in the 4th and 5th verses, is used for the future, which is often the case in prophecies. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF88</span> The word rendered *“renewal,”* means lengthening, that is, of man’s life; hence it is taken in the sense of recovery, — “I will bring to it a recovery and a healing.” See Jeremiah 8:22. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF89</span> The best word for it here, as given by the *Syr.,* is *security;*  “And I will unfold to them abundance of peace and security.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF90</span> So is the *Vulg.,* “suscitabo,” “I will awake,” or rouse; and also the *Sept.* and the *Targ. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF91</span> See Preface to the third volume. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF92</span> It is better to adopt the secondary meaning of the verb, rendered “cut off,” as it is done by the *Syr.* and the *Targ.,* which is that of failing or wanting, — <span style="color:#000080">17. For thus saith Jehovah, —  Not wanting to David shall be a</span> <span style="color:#000080">man, Sitting on the throne of the house of Israel;</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">18. And to the priests, the Levites, Not wanting shall there be a</span> <span style="color:#000080">man before me,  Burning a burnt-offering,  And perfuming an</span> <span style="color:#000080">oblation, And making a sacrifice all the days. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF93</span> The *Tarq.* and the versions, except the *Sept.,* give the idea of domineering or ruling; and this is the meaning of the verb everywhere when followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span> —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">To send away, a man his servant, and a man his maid, The Hebrew</span> <span style="color:#000080">and the Hebrewess, free;  So as not to domineer over them,  A man</span> <span style="color:#000080">over a Jew his brother.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1319 -->

This is a literal rendering of the verse. But perhaps it would be better to render “servant,” bondman; and “maid,” bondwoman, as they were the married as well as the unmarried. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF94</span> It is said afterwards that for *six* years was the servitude to be, and yet the statement here is, “at the *end* of seven years.” Were it not for two other places, (Deuteronomy 15:1; 31:10,) where we find the same words, we might follow the *Sept.* and the *Arab.,* and read *six* instead of *seven.* The Rabbins remove the difneulty by saying that the word, <span style="color:#008080">≈q</span>, means the commencing, as well as the terminating end or extremity; so the meaning then would be, “at the beginning of seven years;” and this would agree with the *six* years afterwards mentioned. And this is the best explanation of the passage. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF95</span> Our version, “at their pleasure,” is the best, or we may render it, “to themselves,” as <span style="color:#008080">çpn</span> often has this meaning. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF96</span> The verb means here evidently to constrain or to force, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And ye have forced them to be to you For bondmen and for</span> <span style="color:#000080">bondwomen.</span></p>

It would be better throughout the passage to retain the words bondmen and bondwomen — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF97</span> The construction of this verse as to “the calf,” is various. Our version is that of *Junius* and *Tremelius.* It is difficult to understand the *Sept.,* the *Targ.,* and the *Vulg.* The *Syr.* is substantially as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">18. And I will make the men who have transgressed my covenant,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Who have not performed the words of the covenant,  Which they</span> <span style="color:#000080">made before me,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*like*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the calf, Which they cut in two and passed</span> <span style="color:#000080">between its parts, —</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">19. The princes of Judah, etc. etc.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">This is the most literal rendering of the passage: the omission of <span style="color:#008080">k</span>, *like,* or as, is not uncommon. — *Ed*.</p>

<!-- PAGE 1320 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftF98</span> It is rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">δυνάστας</span>, rulers,” by the *Sept.,* “eunuchs,” by the *Vulg.* and *Syr.,* and “princes” by the *Targ*. They were the attendants on royalty, not necessarily eunuchs, for Potiphar, who had a wife, was so called. (Genesis 39:7.) They may have acted as judges; and hence perhaps it is, that they are named here with “the priests.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF99</span> So the *Sept.,* the *Vulg.,* and the *Syr.;* and the word, <span style="color:#008080">ãs</span>, has commonly this meaning, a porch, an entrance or a threshold. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF100</span> We see an instance of this in Ahab, 1 Kings 21:27-29. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF101</span> Blaney’s suggestion is not improbable, that the Prophet, having been before tried before the princes, (Jeremiah 26) was put under some restraint, probably forbidden to enter the precincts of the Temple. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF102</span> So the verb is rendered in the *Sept.* and *Vulg.;* but “accepted” by the *Syr.* and *Targ.* Our version is a paraphrase; to convey fully the meaning the word “humbly” ought to have been introduced,” It may be, they will humbly present their supplication,” etc. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF103</span> I render this verse as follows, —

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">8. And Baruch, the son of Neriah, did according to all that Jeremiah</span> <span style="color:#000080">the prophet commanded him, in order to read in the book the</span> <span style="color:#000080">words of Jehovah in the house of Jehovah.</span></p>

What Jeremiah had commanded Baruch was to take a roll and to write the words from his mouth: this Baruch did, and for this purpose, that he might read the words (as the *Targum* has it) in the Lord’s house. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF104</span> The latter part of the verse is differently rendered in the *Sept., Syr.,* and the *Targ.,* and more consistently with the Hebrew, —  <span style="color:#000080">— proclaim a fast before Jehovah did all the people in Jerusalem,</span> <span style="color:#000080">and all the people that came from the cities into Jerusalem.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">It was a fast that the people proclaimed, and not the king, who was a very ungodly one. His conduct on this occasion proved his great impiety. — *Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftF105</span> Some have made two questions here, — “How didst thou write all these words? from his mouth?” The answer seems to favor this construction; as usual in Scripture, the last question is answered first, and then the first: Baruch said, “From his mouth he pronounced to me all these words;” and then he adds, answering the first question, “and I wrote on the book with ink.” — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1321 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftF106</span> There were no chimneys, and therefore no “hearths” in the East. The word rendered “hearth” here means properly a brasier, and <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> before it is a preposition. The verse may be thus rendered, — <span style="color:#000080">And the king was sitting in the winter-house, in the ninth month,</span> <span style="color:#000080">and at the brasier burning (or, which was burning) before him.</span>

It is “a small altar, arula,” in the *Vulg.;* “fire” inthe *Syr.* and *Targ.;* but “hearth” in the *Sept. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftF107</span> The idea of trafficking or buying is given by the *Sept.,* “to buy thence in the midst of the people.” The *Fulg.* is, “that he might divide there *his* possession in the sight of the citizens;” and materially the same meaning is given by the *Syr.*  and the *Targ.* The literal rendering is, “For a portion from thence (or, there) among the people;” which seems to mean, that he intended to go to the land of Benjanmin, that he might get his portion or share from the inheritance he had among his people. So that *Blayney’s* version appears to be right, “to receive a portion thereof among the people.” The Chaldeans had deprived him of his patrimony in the land of Benjamin: when they retreated he purposed to go there, “with the view,” as *Blayney* observes, “of coming in for a share of the produce of the land with the rest of his neighbors.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF108</span> The versions and the *Targ.* render the word differently; its meaning was not evidently understood. *Blaney*  gives the best explanation, who renders it “cells.” “The dungeon,” he says, was a deep pit like a well, and near the bottom were scooped niches, or cells, for the lodgement of the prisoners. The word comes from, <span style="color:#008080">hnh</span>, to fix, to settle, to lodge; hence the plural noun here means places for prisoners to settle or lodge on, where they could fix themselves, without sinking in the mire at the bottom of the pit, as the case was with Jeremiah on another occasion. See Jeremiah 38:6. That pit, it seems, had no cells. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftF109</span> This paragraph is intermingled in the original with the text; but it has been thought better to introduce it separately. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG1</span> “The king,” observes *Blayney,* “evidently speaks this in disgust with the princes, for endeavoring to frustrate his clemency.” — Ed.

<!-- PAGE 1322 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftG2</span> *Blayney* gives a better version, “torn rags and worn-out rags.” The literal rendering is, “Rags of the torn, and rags of the rotten.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftG3</span> The verb means trouble of mind or anxiety rather than fear, “I am disturbed with regard to the Jews,” etc. The *Vulgate* is, “I am solicitous,” and the *Targum.,* “I am anxious. Our version, “I am afraid of,” is the *Syriac.* The king seems to have been too proud to own that he had fear. The last clause in the verse may be thus rendered, “And they exult over me.” The verb means to raise up or elevate one’s self, and then the preposition <span style="color:#008080">b</span> means over, or against. The king was disturbed in his mind, being apprehensive of the taunts and insults of those already gone to the Chaldeans. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG4</span> Both the *Sept.* and the *Vulg.* take *“fixed”* in a transitive sense, “They have fixed” or caused to sink; and the last words are made to refer to the princes. As to the *Vulg.* the two clauses are, “They have sunk thy feet in the mire and in a slippery *place,* and have departed from thee.” The *Syriac.* as to the last clause is the same.

The whole matter is related as seen in a vision, given to the Prophet as he says in the previous verse, — <span style="color:#000080">21. This is the thing which Jehovah made me to see; and (</span><span style="color:#000080">*he said*</span><span style="color:#000080">)</span> <span style="color:#000080">Behold the women, who have remained in the house of Judah, going</span> <span style="color:#000080">forth to the princes of the king of Babylon, and behold them</span> <span style="color:#000080">saying, — They have roused thee and prevailed over thee,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*even*</span> <span style="color:#000080">thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">friends; sunk have they in the mire thy feet, they have run away</span> <span style="color:#000080">from thee.</span>

The scene, as seen in the vision, is presented to the king, the women going out and then speaking tauntingly to him. The princes roused or excited Zedekiah to break faith with the king of Babylon, and prevailed on him to do so. By so doing they sunk him as it were in the mire, that is, brought him to difficulties, and then ran away from him. And then in the next verse the Prophet confirms and explains the vision. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG5</span> And this city shall be burnt with fire,” is the rendering of the *Sept.,* the *Syriac.,* and the *Targum.* The *Vulg.* is, “and he (the king of Babylon) will burn this city with fire.” The first, no doubt, is the true version of the hebrew, except the verb he in *Hiphil,* according to our version, and also that of *Blayney;* but what corresponds best with the passage is the former rendering. — Ed.

<!-- PAGE 1323 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftG6</span> The words literally are, “Let no man know of these words, and thou shalt not die.” Such is the rendering of the *Sept.,* the *Vulg.,* and the *Targum.;* the *Syriac.* is, “lest thou die;” which suggests the view taken by *Calvin. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftG7</span> These words are left out in the *Sept.* and the Syriac. The *Vulgate,* and the *Targum.* give this version, “And it came to pass that Jerusalem was taken;” which seems not in this connection to have any meaning. Some connect them with the following chapter, but improperly. Our version, followed by *Blayney,* gives the best sense, *“And* he was *there* (that is, in the court of the prison)when Jerusalem was taken: “ He was there not only *to* the day or time of its capture, but during that time. This was added to shew that he was not released by the Jews, but by those who took the city. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG8</span> The *pulling out* of his eyes is derived from the *Vulg.;* the other versions and the *Targum.* express literally the Hebrew, “And he blinded the eyes of Zedekiah.” And the custom was to hold before them red-hot iron.
