It seems also that they practiced in the East the horrible custom of pulling out the eyes. But to blind the eyes must have been a different form of barbarity. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG9</span> The *Vulg.* is, “the chief of the soldiers-magister militum*;”* the *Targum.,* “the *prince* of the slayers;” the *Syriac., “the* prince of the attendants, *or* guards.” The best rendering would be, “the chief of the executioners.” The guards, the royal attendants, were commonly the executioners. See 1 Samuel 22:17; Mark 6:27. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG10</span> There is here an oversight; it was his father Ahikam that delivered the Prophet, as recorded in the twenty-sixth chapter (Jeremiah 26). — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG11</span> Why should he kill (or smite) thy life?” so all the versions and the *Targum.* But <span style="color:#008080">çpn</span> means often a corpse or a dead body, Leviticus 21:1; Leviticus 22:4. Then the most obvious rendering would be, “Why should he smite thee dead?” or, “Why should he smite thee a corpse?” *Blayney* gives the meaning, but not a translation,” Wherefore should he take away thy life?*” — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftG12</span> The words may be thus literally rendered, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And Ishmael, the son of Nethaniah, went out from Mizpah to meet</span> <span style="color:#000080">them, walking, walking and weeping, etc.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*He went on foot, and wept as he went out — Ed.*</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1324 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftG13</span> “At the pit,” is the *Sept.; “About* the middle of the pool,” is the *Vulg.* and the *Targum.* It was evidently a ditch or a trench made for the defense of the city. See Jeremiah 41:9*. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftG14</span> It is not redundant, for it is the idiom of the language: and so it is in *Welsh,* though the present version is not correct, which ought to be as follows, — “ A’r clawdd (not pydew) yr hwn y bwriodd Ishmael iddo,” etc. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG15</span> This is in the ninth verse. The words are omitted in the *Sept.; “on* account of Gedaliah,” is the *Vulg.* and the *Targum.;* which is the same with our version. *“Along* with Gedaliah,” is *Blayney’s.* The word “hand,’ often means power, authority, dominion. (Genesis 9:2; Judges 1:35) Then the rendering would be, “on account of the power of Gedaliah;” and this would give the passage the most emphatic meaning: Ishmael smote them because he envied the power given to Gedaliah, which these men, by coming to Mizpah, acknowledged and supported. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG16</span> There was a pool in Gibeon, mentioned in 2 Samuel 2:13; and it must have been large, otherwise it would not have been called “great *waters.” — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftG17</span> It is given as a proper name in the *Sept.;* the idea of peregrination is given in the *Vulgate* and *Targum.* If it be a common noun, its proper meaning is not peregrination, but habitation or dwelling, or rather dwellings, it being in the plural number. *Blayney* takes it as a proper name. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG18</span> Our version is, “to whom we send thee,” and correctly too: literally it is, “whom we send thee to him,” an idiom common in Hebrew and also in *Welsh, “yr* hwn y danfonwn di atto.” The *Vulg.* is, “to whom we send thee;” and so in the *Syriac.* and *Targum.,* but the *Septuagint* tried to imitate the Hebrew, and there is no sense given. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG19</span> The phrase often occurs, and has ever this meaning; and it is the meaning here, no doubt, though the *Sept.* and the *Vulg.* adopt the other sense. The versions often give different senses to the same phrases, which render them unsafe guides. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG20</span> All the versions and the *Targum* differ as to the *construction* of these two verses, the 19th and the 20th, and modern authors too. I offer the following rendering, — <span style="color:#000080">19. The word of Jehovah to you, the remnant of Judah,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is this,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Enter not into Egypt; knowing, know (or, surely know,) that I</span> <span style="color:#000080">make this pro-</span>

<!-- PAGE 1325 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">20. test to you this day. Verily, ye do go greatly astray against</span> <span style="color:#000080">your own selves; for ye sent me to Jehovah your God, etc., etc.</span></p>

The first clause is according to the *Vulg.* The express message was, not to enter into Egypt. What they were to know and remember was the protest he made to them. Then in verse 20th, he charges them with inconsistency, that they went astray from their own professions, and afterwards he specifies what they had promised. There is, according to this view, a consistency in the whole passage. The word *soul* is often taken for the person: “against your own selves,” is literally “against your own souls.” The meaning of the phrase is, that they belied themselves, as it is evident from what follows. The past tense in Hebrew may often be rendered by the present, as it refers to time up to the present and including the present. The future also in Hebrew may be rendered by the present, because it refers often to what is now and continues to *be. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftG21</span> It is rendered by the *Sept. and Syr.,* “arms;” by the *Vulg.,* “throne;” by the *Targum.,* “tent.” Strange that there should be such a difference. It was something to be stretched out or extended, for such is the meaning of the verb; and it was something beautiful, for so the word[means. It was probably a canopy erecton over the throne, which was to be set on *the stones. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftG22</span> The first verb is rendered in the third person, by the *Sept.,* the *Vulg.,* and the *Syriac.,* “He will kindle;” but in the first by the *Targum.,* “I will kindle.” The third person runs better with the context; but if a causative sense be given to the verb, it will be equally the same, *“And* I will cause *him* to kindle a fire in the houses of the gods of Egypt, and he will burn them, and carry them away captive; that is, he will burn the parts made of wood, and carry away the gold and the silver; “and he will put on the land of Egypt as the shepherd puts on his coat,” that is, he will put on the spoils of the land with the same ease and facility as the shepherd puts on his coat, and carry them away, no one molesting or hindering him. — Ed.

<!-- PAGE 1326 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftG23</span> The easiest way to reconcile this seeming inconsistency is as follows: He threatens the fugitives to Egypt with the sword; this sword was that of Nebuchadnezzar, as he foretells in Jeremiah 46:15, etc. None would escape this sword except those who might have escaped in the meantime into the land of Judah. We see the same thing referred to in Jeremiah 46:28; and that verse may be thus rendered, — And those who shall escape the sword (*who* shall have returned from the land of Egypt to the land of Judah) shall be few in number; but all the remnant of Judah, who have gone to the land of Egypt to sojourn there, shall know the word, which shall stand, *what is* from me or from them. — Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftG24</span> It is more consistent with the passage to render the verbs in the future tense, — <span style="color:#000080">Not flee shall the swift, Nor escape shall the strong; In the north,</span> <span style="color:#000080">by the side of the river Euphrates, Have they stumbled and fallen.</span> <span style="color:#000080">— Ed.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftG25</span> The *city* here is put in opposition to the *land, —* And it (Egypt) said, I will ascend, I will cover the land; I will destroy (every) city, and the dwellers in it.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH1</span> All the versions, except the *Syriac,* which *Calvin* has followed, have “to Moab,” and connect the words with the following, that is, “Jehovah says thus to Moab.” The best version is, as given by *Blayney* and *Henderson,* “concerning Moab, thus saith,” etc.. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH2</span> Some give this rendering, “Alas! no Nebo;” it had ceased to exist, and the reason is given, “for it is laid waste. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH3</span> Neither the *Vulg.* nor the *Syr.* gives this as a proper name, nor is there any such place found elsewhere. *Blayney* renders it “the high fortress,” agreeably with the *Vulg., Syr.,* and the *Targ. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH4</span> None of the versions renders this a proper name, but as a participle from the verb which follows, and no such place is mentioned elsewhere. They must have read <span style="color:#008080">hmrm</span>, instead of <span style="color:#008080">ˆmdm</span>. Then the version would be, <span style="color:#000080">Even silenced thou shalt be silenced,</span> <span style="color:#000080">After thee shall go the sword.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">To be silenced, in the language of the prophets, is to be subdued. See</p>

Isaiah 15:1, when the same thing is said of Moab. The word

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">silence forms a contrast with the boasting of Moab mentioned at the beginning of the verse. After being subdued and removed elsewhere,</p>

<!-- PAGE 1327 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">still the sword would follow Moab. — Ed.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH5</span> Here all the versions and the *Targum* differ. The *Vulg.* only has “little ones;” the *Syr.* has “her poor,” the *Sept.* take “Zoar” to be intended, according to Isaiah 15:5, the word <span style="color:#008080">hr[wx</span>, instead of <span style="color:#008080">hyrw[x</span>. The passage in Isaiah confirms this reading, though not found in any copies. Then the verse would read thus, — <span style="color:#000080">Broken is Moab, They made the cry heard at Zoar.</span>

This is substantially the version of *Venema. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH6</span> The word *enemies* is given only by the *Vulg.;* the other versions render it “distress.” The literal rendering of the verse is,<span style="color:#000080">For</span> <span style="color:#000080">*in*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the ascent to Luhith,  With weeping ascends weeping;  For</span> <span style="color:#000080">in the descent to Heronaim, The distress of the cry of ruin have</span> <span style="color:#000080">they heard.</span>

This version materially corresponds with Isaiah 15:5. Weeping ascending with weeping, shews that all wept as they ascended. “The distress of the cry” is a Hebraism for distressing cry*. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH7</span> “Incliners” is the *Sept.;* “strewers,” the *Vulg*.; “plunderers,” the *Syr.* and *Targ.* The verb means to spread, to strew. They were those who turned the wine vessels in order to empty them. *Henderson* has “overturners;” but *Blayney* has the best word, “tilters,” who should tilt him. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH8</span> There is no agreement in the Versions and *Targ.,* as to these words, nor among critics. The easiest construction is presented by *Blayney, —* <span style="color:#000080">A spoiler of Moab and of her cities is gone up.</span>

The next clause is not so well rendered by *Blayney.* He applies it to the Chaldeans. “Moab” is spoken of in this chapter, both in the feminine and in the masculine gender. In our language the neuter would be the most suitable, it and its. I render the verse thus, — <span style="color:#000080">15. The waster of Moab and of its cities</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">going up, And the</span> <span style="color:#000080">choice of its youth shall descend to the slaughter,  Saith the King,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah of hosts is his name.</span>

“Going up” as ascribed to the conqueror, and “descending” to the conquered. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1328 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftH9</span> The literal rendering is, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">How has the rod of strength been broken, The staff of honor?</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">“How” is by what means, or how much: the first seems to be the meaning here. The rod and the staff are the same — the sceptre an ensign of power and of honor or glory. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH10</span> The verb “dwell” favors the idea adopted by some, that <span style="color:#008080">amx</span> means here a dry or thirsty land. - *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH11</span> <span style="color:#008080">hl[</span> here, as in Jeremiah 48:15, is a participle, and so the verb which follows. The “waster” is represented as then on his way, — <span style="color:#000080">For the waster of Moab is ascending against thee, The destroyer of</span> <span style="color:#000080">thy fortresses. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH12</span> The word has no other meaning than that of smiting, striking, or clapping the hand. A drunkard rejoices by clapping his hands, even in his filth, and thus makes himself an object of ridicule and derision; or he may strike his hands in agony: but it is by the first he renders himself ridiculous, the thing evidently intended here. It is observed justly by *Blayney,* that the first verb in the verse is in the singular number, used for the plural; and he regarded this verb to be the same; and his version is, — <span style="color:#000080">And clap at Moab in his vomiting.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The objection to this is, the verb in this sense is not used without a preposition after it; see Lamentations 2:15: otherwise this would suit the passage: it was suggested by *Gataker. - Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH13</span> The *Vulg.* and the *Targ.* give the best version of these words, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Surely for the abundance of thy words against him, thou shalt be</span> <span style="color:#000080">quickly removed,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*or,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">led captive.</span></p>

Then, in the following verse, Moab is bidden to quit his cities *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH14</span> Our version in Isaiah 16:6, where the same form of words occurs, is, “We have heard of,” though here the “of” is dropped, and thus the meaning of *Calvin* is conveyed, which is favored by the early versions.

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">The verse may be thus literally rendered, —</p>

<!-- PAGE 1329 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">We have heard the arrogance of Moab; Very arrogant</span> <span style="color:#000080">*has been*</span> <span style="color:#000080">his</span> <span style="color:#000080">insolence and arrogance; Yea, his arrogantness and the loftiness of</span> <span style="color:#000080">his heart.</span></p>

The word for “arrogance” means swelling; it is to grow big, and to claim more than what belongs to us. Then “insolence” signifies to rise high, so as to look down on others with contempt. Arrogance is first, then insolence: and in the last line the two are inverted, and with this difference, the disposition is denoted in the last line, and the acting in the former. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH15</span> Ira furor brevis est*. — Epist. 2 ad Loll.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH16</span> The versions and the *Targ.* all differ as to this verse. The *Vulg.* is the best; it takes <span style="color:#008080">µydb</span>, branches, and also limbs, in a metaphorical sense, signifying strength. I give the following rendering, — <span style="color:#000080">30. I know, saith Jehovah, his excess, (</span><span style="color:#000080">*i.e.*</span> <span style="color:#000080">of pride;) But not so his</span> <span style="color:#000080">strength, not so have they done.</span>

The mixture of numbers, singular and plural, is common in the prophets — “his” and “they.” The meaning seems to be, that however excessive was the pride and insolence of Moab, they had no power fully to effect their purposes. - *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH17</span> This paragraph has been transplanted from the text.

The verbs here are imperatives in the *Sept* and *Syr.*, “Howl ye,” etc.; and in the future tense in the *Targ.,* “they shall howl, etc.  The *Vulg.,* is according to the Hebrew. The last verb is in the third person, “He (Moab) will mourn for the men of Kir-heres.” This city was on the extremity of Moab northward, as Jazer was on its extremity southward. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH18</span> Jam canit extremos effœtus vinitor antes. — *Geor*. 2:417.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH19</span> A reason more suitable to the passage has been given for this comparison, — that Moab in its distress is compared to an heifer lowing for want of pasture and especially of water, for it follows that the waters of Nimrim would be dried up. See Isaiah 15:5, 6*. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH20</span> As to this clause, widely different are all the versions; the *Targ.* gives the general sense. The word <span style="color:#008080">trty</span> is evidently plural, the <span style="color:#008080">w</span> being

<!-- PAGE 1330 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">wanting. “Reserves,” as given by *Blayney,* is an exact rendering, — <span style="color:#000080">Because the reserves he had made have perished.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Connected with this word is another in Isaiah 15:7, which means “deposits;” both signify the wealth or treasures they had laid up. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH21</span> All the versions and *Targ.* read, “as a useless vessel;” but the Hebrew is, “as a vessel without delight in it,” *i.e.,* as a vessel which has nothing pleasing or agreeable in it. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH22</span> The literal rendering is as follows, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">How broken! they howled; How has Moab turned the back</span> <span style="color:#000080">ashamed! Thus Moab has become a derision And a terror to all</span> <span style="color:#000080">around him.</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">The past tense is used for the future. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH23</span> The literal rendering of the verse is as follows, the nominative case to the two verbs being Moab, taken here as the country, — <span style="color:#000080">41. Taken it is, — the cities; And the strongholds, — it is seized:</span> <span style="color:#000080">And become shall the heart of the valiants of Moab, In that day,</span> <span style="color:#000080">like the heart of a woman in distress.</span>

In our language it would be, “as to its cities,” and, “as to its strongholds.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH24</span> There is a striking alliteration in these words, fear, pit, snare — *peched, pechet, pech. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH25</span> The word “strength” is here omitted. Calvin’s version is, “Under the shadow of Heshbon stood they who had fled from strength,” or violence, *i.e.,* of their enemies. Some connect it with “stood,” the fugitives “stood for strength,” or, “without strength,” which, perhaps, is preferable: they stood under the shadow or protection of Heshbon, and obtained no help; so far was this from being the case, that from Heshbon would go forth fire, that is, “the spoiler,” or, destroyer, before often mentioned. Then <span style="color:#008080">yk</span> would have its usual meaning, *for,* as giving a reason why the fugitives remained without strength or help, under the protection of Heshbon. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1331 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftH26</span> Most give a different explanation of this fire, that it designated “the spoiler” that was to come on Moab. That fire has often this meaning is evident. See Judges 9:20. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH27</span> The last clause is evidently a quotation from Numbers 24:17: it is not literally the same, but the meaning is so. It is “corner”’ here and not “corners,” as in Numbers; and the word there is <span style="color:#008080">rqrq</span>, and not <span style="color:#008080">dqdq</span> as here, only there are some copies which have the former word here. In that case, the passage would read thus, — <span style="color:#000080">And it shall devour the corner of Moab, And destroy the sons (or</span> <span style="color:#000080">children) of tumult.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#008080">tç</span> in Numbers is probably for <span style="color:#008080">taç</span>, which means the same as the word here used, coming from the same root, and properly rendered “tumult.”</p>

This passage is omitted in the *Sept.;* the *Vulg.* renders <span style="color:#008080">dqdq</span>, “verticem,” the crown or top of the head; but the *Syr.* and *Targ.* drop the metaphor, and render it “chiefs” or nobles. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH28</span> Literally it is, “To the children of Ammon thus saith Jehovah:” so the *Sept.,* the *Vulg.,* and the *Targ.* There are prophecies concerning Ammon in Ezekiel 21:28-32; 25:2-7; Amos 1:13-15; and in  Zephaniah 2:8-11. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH29</span> “Milcom” is given by the *Sept.,* the *Vulg.,* and the *Syr.;* but “their king” by the *Targ.* In Amos 1:15, the *Vulg.* and *Syr*. are the same; but the *Sept.* have “kings,” and the *Targ.* is the same as here. There was a king of Ammon, Jeremiah 27:3; and there is one passage in which the possession of a country is ascribed to a heathen god, to Chemosh, see Judges 11:24. But *“inheriting”* is more suitably applied to a king than to an idol; and the contrast in the next verse is with Israel and not with God, *“Israel* shall be heir,” etc. Most probably, then, the king is meant, and not the idol. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH30</span> The quotation is not literally given, but the meaning of the passage. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH31</span> Literally it is, “And Israel shall inherit his inheritors.” The Ammonites claimed to be the heirs, and Israel succeeded them as the right heir. This prophecy was fulfilled as recorded in 1 Macc. 5:6, 7, 28-36. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH32</span> Except in 1 Macc. 5:6-8. The victories of the Maccabees were, no doubt, a literal accomplishment of this prophecy. See verses 33, 34;

<!-- PAGE 1332 -->

where the sound of the “trumpets” is expressly mentioned. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH33</span> The verb means to flow out, and to flow away, to waste. The latter seems to be the meaning here, “wasted has thy valley.” (See Lamentations 4:9.) It has a noun after it, when it means to flow out in the sense of abounding; but when used intransitively, it means to flow away in the sense of wasting, — <span style="color:#000080">Why gloriest thou in deep valleys! Flown away has thy deep</span> <span style="color:#000080">valley, O daughter, who hast turned aside, Who hast trusted in thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">treasures, Who hast said in thine heart, “Who can come to me?”</span>

The participle <span style="color:#008080">hbbwçh</span>, “who hast turned aside,” or away, is rendered “delicate,” by the *Vulg.,* and “beloved,” by the *Syr.,* and the idea of impudence or folly, is conveyed by the *Sept.* and *Targ*. How the word could be so rendered, it is difficult to say. The verb means to turn to or from. Being a reduplicate here, it means to turn away resolutely; hence “rebellious” would be no improper rendering. “Her” before “treasures,” refers to “daughter,” but in our language “thy” reads better, as adopted by the *Vulg.* and the *Syr.* There is an addition in several copies of the words, “Who hast said in thine heart,” and all the versions have what corresponds with them. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH34</span> So the *Vulg.* and the *Targ.,* while the *Sept.* and the *Syr.,* have “prudent,” or intelligent. The word is not in its regular form, the y *iod* being wanted, and the m *mem* before it being omitted, which is not uncommon. Discerning rather than “prudent,” or “intelligent,” is its meaning. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH35</span> Some maintain that the first clause only is a question, for there is no interrogatory particle prefixed to the other clauses, — <span style="color:#000080">Is wisdom no longer in Teman? Perished has counsel from the</span> <span style="color:#000080">discerning, Vanished has their wisdom.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1333 -->
Neither the versions nor the *Targum* put the two last lines as questions; nor the *Sept.* and the *Syr.* the first. The verb <span style="color:#008080">jrs</span> is differently rendered, — by the *Sept.,* “departed;” by the *Vulg.,* “become useless;” by the *Syr.,* “taken away;” by the *Targ.,* “marred,” or corrupted. The verb means to spread, to stretch out; and spreading here is in the sense of dissipating or scattering, and the verb here is passive. So “vanished” would convey the meaning. The first line is a question, and the two following contain the answer. A tautology cannot be otherwise avoided. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH36</span> The versions, (except the *Syr.* and the *Targ.*) have the verbs here in the imperative mood; and they are so regarded by most critics, “Flee ye, turn back, dwell deep,” etc.. This is necessary on account of <span style="color:#008080">yk</span>, *for,* which follows. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH37</span> The interpreters probably referred to are the *Sept.* and the *Vulg.,* where the interrogative form is not used; not so the *Syr.* and the *Targ. — Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH38</span> Neither of the two explanations here given are satisfactory, though the first especially has been adopted by many, such as *Henry* and *Scott.* It is difficult to know the meaning of the *Sept.;* the *Vulg.* and the *Syr.* are literally our version. The *Targ.* goes wide astray, representing this verse as addressed to the people of Israel, of whom there is no mention here. *Blayney* supposes a typographical mistake, joins <span style="color:#008080">bz[</span> to the preceding verse, and puts <span style="color:#008080">h</span>, to the next word, and gives this version, — <span style="color:#000080">And there is nothing of him left. 11. Shall I preserve the life of thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">fatherless children? Or shall thy widows trust in me?</span>

The questions he considers as strong negatives. The simpler view seems to be this: in the preceding verse the destruction not only of Esau, but also of his brethren and neighbours, is announced. His “seed” means his posterity, the nation, and he was *was not to be,* that is, as a kingdom. There would be still some “orphans” and “widows,” and as “brethren” and “neighbors” would be destroyed as well as Esau himself, as to all grown up people, forming the nation, and thus orphans and widows would be left helpless, God was pleased to give the promise here stated: <span style="color:#000080">Leave thy orphans, I will preserve</span> <span style="color:#000080">*them,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Thy widows also, in me</span> <span style="color:#000080">let them trust.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The last verb is both masculine and feminine, and refers both to the orphans and widows. This is substantially the explanation given by Venema, and is the most satisfactory. — *Ed*.</p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#800000">ftH39</span> It is better to render it as in the *Syr.,* “astonishment;” then “reproach”</p>

<!-- PAGE 1334 -->

comes after it; and the next word, <span style="color:#008080">brj</span>, is properly “a waste,” and in the plural is rendered “wastes” at the end of the verse. There were two cities called Bozrah, one in Moab, Jeremiah 48:24, and one in Edom, Isaiah 63:1. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH40</span> This verse is variously rendered in the versions and in the *Targum,* and also by commentators. The following rendering I deem plain and literal, — <span style="color:#000080">Behold, as a lion from the swelling of Jordan, Will he ascend to the</span> <span style="color:#000080">strong habitation; For suddenly will I cause him to run from it: And</span> <span style="color:#000080">he who is chosen will I appoint over her; For who is like me? and</span> <span style="color:#000080">who can meet me? And who is he, the shepherd, who can stand</span> <span style="color:#000080">before me?</span>

The word <span style="color:#008080">h[ygra</span>, as in Proverbs 12:19, is “suddenly,” or in a moment. “Him” is the lion, and “from it,” the swelling of Jordan. “Over her” is Edom. “Who can meet me?” that is, to contend with me, or resist *me,* according to the *Sept.* The verb is <span style="color:#008080">d[y</span>, though *Calvin* derived it from <span style="color:#008080">dw[</span>. The “shepherd” is mentioned, because of the “lion,” whom no shepherd can resist when he attacks the flock. God speaks of himself as identified with his chosen one*. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH41</span> The literal rendering is, —

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">The cry — at the Red Sea was heard its sound.</span></p>

It is an instance of the nominative case absolute. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH42</span> There are several copies in which the <span style="color:#008080">k</span>, *caph,* is found, and it is evidently the most suitable reading, — <span style="color:#000080">Confounded is Hamath and Arpad; For an evil report have they</span> <span style="color:#000080">heard, — they melt away; Like the sea the agitation, the quieting</span> <span style="color:#000080">none can effect.</span>

The melting away was through fear. They were moved or agitated, and, like the sea, they could not rest or be still. <span style="color:#008080">la</span> may be often rendered none or nothing. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH43</span> So the versions, (except the *Sept*.) and also the *Targ.,* the <span style="color:#008080">y</span> *iod* being regarded as paragogic. So *Grotius. - Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH44</span> The *Syr.* rendered it “spared;” the *Vulg.* has left out the negative *not.*

<!-- PAGE 1335 -->

There are two difficulties, the verb <span style="color:#008080">bz[</span> is not used in the sense of sparing, though *Gataker* labours to shew this; then the connexion: the next verse begins with a “therefore.” Take the verb here in its usual sense, and then “therefore” will have its force. He asks, Why was not this city, under the circumstances previously mentioned, forsaken or abandoned, notwithstanding its being a celebrated and a joyous city? But as it was not forsaken, “therefore,” he says, “her young men shall fall in her streets,” etc.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">*Venema’s* view is different; his version is, — <span style="color:#000080">Why not? forsaken has been the city of praise, The city of my joy.</span></p>

That is, Why should not Damascus be compelled to flee, since Jerusalem had been forsaken, the city of praise and of his joy. “Therefore,” in this sense, refers to the slaughter of those who would not flee. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH45</span> It is *“Kedem”* in the *Sept.,* and *“East”* in the other versions and the *Targum. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH46</span> This verb is deemed by most to be in the imperative mood, like the two foregoing verbs; and it is so given in the *Sept.,* the *Vulg.,* and the *Targ.* In the *Syr*., all the verbs are in the past tense, which is not consistent with the context. *Blayney’s* version is, — <span style="color:#000080">Flee ye, move off apace, Retire deep for to dwell, etc.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The meaning is, as he says, that they should go into deep caverns to hide themselves from their enemies. See Judges 6:2; 1 Samuel 13:6. *— Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH48</span> The difference in the two clauses is properly distinguished by *Blayney,* in his version, — <span style="color:#000080">There shall not a man dwell there, Nor shall a son of man sojourn</span> <span style="color:#000080">therein. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH49</span> They were the descendants of Elam the son of Shem, Genesis 10:22. They were a powerful kingdom in the days of Abram, Genesis 14:1. Isaiah speaks of them as hostile to the people of

<!-- PAGE 1336 -->
Israel, Isaiah 22:6. Shushan is said to have been in the province of Elam, Daniel 8:2*. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH50</span> “The indignation of his wrath” is in apposition with “evil.” So the *Vulg.,* the *Syr.,* and the *Targ.* <span style="color:#000080">And I will bring on them evil, The burning of my wrath, saith</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah.</span>

The evil was the effect of God’s high displeasure. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH51</span> Most consider that “Merodach” here was a false god; first probably a king, afterwards deified. As confounded, or put to shame, is applied to Bel, the other verb <span style="color:#008080">tj</span>, should be rendered “dismayed” or terrified, a meaning which it often has, — <span style="color:#000080">Taken is Babylon, Confounded is Bel, Terrified is Merodach;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Confounded are her images, Terrified are her idols.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The word for “images” means labor, and refers to the labor and pains taken by those who made them; and the word for “idols” means a trunk or log of wood from which they were made. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH52</span> The two verbs are rendered by the versions, except the *Syr*., and by the *Targ.,* in the future tense, “They shall come and be joined,” etc.; and one MS., says *Blayney,* has <span style="color:#008080">waby</span>. This would read better. Then the verse would. run thus — <span style="color:#000080">To Zion will they ask the way, Hither their faces; They shall come</span> <span style="color:#000080">and be joined to Jehovah, By an everlasting covenant,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*which*</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">not be forgotten.</span>

“Hither” and not “thither,” for the Prophet was at Jerusalem; and so the particle means, and it is so given in the *Sept.* and *Vulg.* The last clause requires “which” in our translation, though not in *Welsh,* for, like the Hebrew, it can do without it — *nad anghofir.* literally the Hebrew. What is here predicted was literally accomplished, as recorded by Nehemiah, (Nehemiah 9:38; Nehemiah 10:29.) — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH53</span> I render the verse thus, —

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">6. Lost sheep have become my people; Their shepherds have</span> <span style="color:#000080">caused them to err, Having turned them here and there on the</span> <span style="color:#000080">mountains; From mountain to hill have they gone; They have</span> <span style="color:#000080">forgotten their resting-place.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1337 -->
The meaning of <span style="color:#008080">µybbwç</span> is given by the *Sept.* and *Vulg.,* “causing them to wander;” the verb <span style="color:#008080">bç</span> is to turn; being here a *reduplicate,* it means to turn much, or again and again, or here and there; and this is confirmed by what follows — they went, through the teaching of their pastors, from “mountain to hill,” that is, from one form of idolatry to another; and “forgotten their resting-place,” which was God. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH54</span> *Calvin,* in his exposition of Isaiah 37:26, applies what is said to Sion, and not to Sennacherib, as it is commonly done. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH55</span> The most approved exposition is the first, which makes the latter words to be in apposition with Jehovah, as given in the Versions, though the last clause seems to be a separate sentence, — <span style="color:#000080">Because they have sinned against Jehovah, The habitation of</span> <span style="color:#000080">righteousness; And the hope of their fathers</span> <span style="color:#000080">*was*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah.</span>

By calling God the habitation of righteousness, what is implied is, as *Lowth* suggests, that they would not have been banished, had they not justly deserved to be so treated, God being the seat or dwelling-place of justice or righteousness. And in addition to this, he had been the hope of their fathers. See Jeremiah 40:3, where we have an example of what their enemies alleged. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH56</span> Their arrows, like those of a skilful warrior*. — Blayney.* In the next line there is an instance of <span style="color:#008080">al</span> being in the sense of *none,–* “None shall return empty,” or void; i.e., without effecting its purpose. See Isaiah 55:11. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH57</span> Several copies have <span style="color:#008080">hçd</span>, threshing. Being allowed to eat at pleasure, the threshing heifer became fat and frisky. It is so taken by *Blayney* and *Henderson,* though not countenanced by the Versions or the *Targ. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH58</span> Jeremiah having twice before (Jeremiah 8:16; Jeremiah 47:3) used the word for steeds or horses, we may conclude that he means the same here. — *Ed.*

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#800000">ftH59</span> The connection of these two verses will be more evident, if we render</p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#008080">yk</span> *when,* as proposed by *Gataker,* and not *surely,* as by *Calvin,* nor *because,* as in our version, — <span style="color:#000080">11. When ye shall rejoice, when ye shall exult, Ye plunderers of</span> <span style="color:#000080">mine heritage,  When ye shall skip as a fed heifer, And neigh like</span> <span style="color:#000080">steeds,</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1338 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">12. Ashamed greatly</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall be*</span> <span style="color:#000080">your mother, Confounded</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall*</span> <span style="color:#000080">she</span> <span style="color:#000080">be who bare you; Behold, the last of the nations</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall she be,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">A</span> <span style="color:#000080">desert, a dry land, and a wilderness.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The reference seems to be to the rejoicings of Babylon, when it was taken. — *Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH60</span> Or literally, “Because against Jehovah has she sinned.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH61</span> Rather “battlements” or ramparts, as given by the *Sept.;* for it is not proper to say that “*foundations*” have fallen. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH62</span> Literally, “and boned him;” which is to be taken in a privative sense, “and unboned him.” There are similar words in Hebrew: to neck is to break the neck. (Exodus 13:13.) To tail is to cut off the tail. (Joshua 10:19.) To root is to root up. (Psalm 52:5.) The *Vulg.* here is *exossavit. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH63</span> The idea of this verse is rightly given in these words: the punishment for iniquity and sins would not be exacted, because God would pardon the remnant; hence they appeared not. The removal of punishment, the restoration from exile, would shew that iniquity and sins no longer existed, God having fully pardoned them, and thus obliterated them.

The iniquity of Israel was false worship, the worship of the calves, and the sins of Judah were especially idolatry and the rejection of God’s messages by his prophets. For these evils more particularly they were banished, and their exile proved a remedy for them, as they never afterwards fell into these sins. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH64</span> Merathaim and Pekod are appellatives, and not proper names, in the early versions, and the first is so in the *Targ*. and rendered “rebellious;” but by the *Sept*. “bitterly;” by the *Vulg.* “rulers;” and by the *Syr.* “exasperating.” The most probable derivation of the word is from <span style="color:#008080">hrm</span>, to rebel, with a dual termination, doubly rebellious, *i.e.,* very rebellious. As to “Pekod,” the versions give it the idea of visiting by way of punishment: “Avenge thou with the sword,” is the *Sept.;* “Visit her inhabitants,” the *Vulg.;* “Assail ye her and her inhabitants,” the *Syr.;* the *Targ.* has “the inhabitants of Pekod.” It is better to take both words as appellatives: — <span style="color:#000080">21. Against the land of the most rebellious, against her ascend, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">to the inhabitants of visitation; Slay and utterly destroy their</span>

<!-- PAGE 1339 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">posterity, saith Jehovah, And do according to all that I have</span> <span style="color:#000080">commanded thee.</span></p>

As to Babylon being “rebellious,” see Jeremiah 50:24, 33. “Inhabitants of visitation” were such as were to be visited, *i.e.,* with judgment; see Jeremiah 50:31. The repetition, “against her,” is emphatical. “posterity,” *i.e.,* children, or young men, as in Jeremiah 50:30. See 1 Kings 16:3. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH65</span> The first verb, <span style="color:#008080">[dg</span>, means to cast off as well as to cut off; the breaking is expressed by the following verb. According to order often found in the Prophets, the final act, casting off, is first mentioned, and then the previous act, the breaking of it, — <span style="color:#000080">23. How has the hammer of all the earth Been cast off and broken!</span> <span style="color:#000080">How has Babylon become a wonder among nations!</span>

“A wonder” or astonishment, for so the word is evidently to be taken here, according to the *Syr.,* though rendered “extinction” by the *Sept.,* and “desert” by the *Vulg.* and *Targ. Blayney* and *Henderson* render it “astonishment.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH66</span> “Treasury” here means an armory or arsenal, as rendered by *Blayney:* and then “instruments” signify weapons, drawn from the armory. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH67</span> Literally it is, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">For a work — this the Lord Jehovah of hosts has In the land of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">Chaldeans. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1340 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftH68</span> It is added, “from the extremity,” *i.e.,* of heaven, according to a parallel passage in Isaiah 13:5. They were to come from the farthest parts of the earth then known. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH69</span> The most approved rendering is, “Cast (or throw) her up as heaps,” *i.e.,* of rubbish, according to Jeremiah 51:37. It is said that the verb here never means to tread under foot, “Make her, of a goodly, stately city, nothing but heaps of earth, stones, and rubbish.” *Gataker. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH70</span> The *Sept.* and *Syr.* take <span style="color:#008080">yrp</span> here as signifying fruit, *i.e.,* of the womb — children, offspring; and what is said in Isaiah 13:16-18, favours this meaning, as well as what is said in Jeremiah 50:21, if we render <span style="color:#008080">µhyrja</span>, “their posterity.” The *Vulg.* and the *Targ.* render the word here “valiants,” expressing the meaning of “bullocks.” The first version is the most suitable, — <span style="color:#000080">Slay ye all her fruit (or offspring;)</span> <span style="color:#000080">Let them descend to the slaughter.</span>

It is descending to the slaughter that led critics to render <span style="color:#008080">yrp</span> bullocks, but we find this expression unconnected with bullocks in Jeremiah 48:15; where “chosen young men” are said to

“descend to the slaughter.” To descend denotes degradation, and to ascend dignity. The *Targ.* has, “Let them be delivered to the slaughter.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH71</span> The early versions and the *Targ.* render <span style="color:#008080">µybr</span>, “*many*;” and the rendering of the *Sept.* and *Vulg.* is to this effect, — <span style="color:#000080">Proclaim ye to the many at Babylon, To all who bend the bow, —</span> <span style="color:#000080">“Encompass her around, Let there be no escape,” etc.</span>

The first part is a charge like what we find in the second verse; and the second states what they were to do. “Proclaim ye to,” is literally, “‘Make ye to hear,” — “Make ye the many at Babylon to hear,” etc. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH72</span> The versions, except the *Syr.* and the *Targ.,* give a similar meaning to this verb: but there is no instance of the verb in Hiphil having this meaning, though it would be the most suitable to this place. At the same time we may consider the land of Chaldea to be meant, if we regard the stillness or quietness as referring to the check produced by God’s restraining power, — <span style="color:#000080">So as to render the land still,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And to terrify the inhabitants of Babylon.</span>

The promise is to make the land quiet so as not to oppose the return of the Jews, and for the same purpose, to terrify Babylon. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH73</span> The word is rendered “diviners” by the *Vulg.,* the *Syr.,* and the *Targ.;* it is left out by the *Sept.* Some derive it from <span style="color:#008080">adb</span>, to feign, to devise, to invent, the <span style="color:#008080">a</span> being left out in <span style="color:#008080">µydb</span>, others say that it comes from <span style="color:#008080">db</span>, alone, solitary, separate, so that <span style="color:#008080">µydb</span> were the recluse, retirement

<!-- PAGE 1341 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">or seclusion being often the habit of impostors. It is connected, in</p>

<!-- PAGE 1342 -->
Isaiah 44:25, with <span style="color:#008080">µymsq</span>, diviners, prognosticators. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH74</span> Events would prove, that they were foolish and ignorant, being not able to foretell the ruin of their own nation, notwithstanding their boast in the knowledge of futurity. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH75</span> It is so rendered by the *Vulg*. and the *Targ.,* omitted by the *Sept.,* and rendered “sword” by the *Syr.,* and also by *Blayney* and *Henderson.* The same incongruity exists as to the treasures; but the sword here, means those who wielded it, the soldiers of Cyrus: they spoiled the treasures, they turned the streams of the rivers. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH76</span> That the Babylonians had large idols or images, which were of terrific size, is evident from Daniel 3:1 — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH77</span> As to the two first words, the versions and the *Targ.* widely differ. According to *Bochart,* the first word, <span style="color:#008080">µyyx</span>, means “wild cats,” and the second, <span style="color:#008080">µyya</span>, jackals. The *Vulg.,* the *Syr*., and the *Targ.* render the other word, <span style="color:#008080">hn[y</span>, the same, “the ostrich;” and this is the general opinion. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH78</span> Rather “*sojourn,*” according to the *Sept. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH79</span> Rather “spear” or lance; so the *Sept.* and *Syr*., though the *Vulg.* and *Targ.* have “shield.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH80</span> See note on Jeremiah 49:19. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH81</span> The *Targ.* and the versions widely differ from one another. The cabbalistic solution is very frivolous, by which the two words <span style="color:#008080">bl</span> and <span style="color:#008080">ymq</span> are made one, and made to signify “Chaldeans,” according to what was called “Athbash,” by which *aleph,* the first letter, was taken for *tau,* the last letter, and *beth,* the second, for *shin,* the last but one; and so on through the whole alphabet. But *Blayney* and others, such as *Gataker* and *Venema,* give a satisfactory explanation of the words. The word <span style="color:#008080">bl</span>, the heart, often means the middle of anything, as “the heart of heaven,” in Deuteronomy 4:11 means the midst of heaven; and “the heart of the seas,” in Psalm 46:2, means the midst of the seas. So here, “the heart of my adversaries,” means the centre of the country of his adversaries. *i.e.,* Babylon, — <span style="color:#000080">Against the inhabitants of the metropolis</span> <span style="color:#000080">of my adversaries. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span> <span style="color:#800000">ftH82</span> It is singular that <span style="color:#008080">la</span> is omitted in the *Sept.* and the *Syr.,* and retained in its negative sense in the *Vulg.* and the *Targ.,* which makes no sense consistently with the context. There is evidently <span style="color:#008080">rça</span> understood before the first verb, as is often the case when the verb is in the future tense. Then the literal rendering would be this, — <span style="color:#000080">At him who bends let the bender bend his bow, And at him who</span> <span style="color:#000080">glories in his coat of mail; And spare ye not her chosen men,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Utterly destroy all her host.</span>

There is here perfect consistency. They who take <span style="color:#008080">la</span> as a negative say, that the first part is addressed to the Chaldeans, and the second to their enemies; but this would be strangely abrupt. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftH83</span> The explanation of the last clasue is, according to the Jewish commentators, not generally taken. The “land,” by most is deemed to be the land of Israel and Judah. The word <span style="color:#008080">µça</span>, means not only guilt, but also guilt’s penalty — judgement; and this seems to be its meaning here, — <span style="color:#000080">For not widowed is Israel, By his God, by Jehovah of hosts;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Though their land has been filled With judgement by the Holy One</span> <span style="color:#000080">of Israel.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">But if we render <span style="color:#008080">m</span> *before* or *against*, then the last line would be, —</p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">With guilt (or sin) before the Holy One of Israel. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH84</span> Some render the last word “reel,” or stagger, and perhaps more consistently with the comparison of drunkenness. The verb in *Hithpael*, as here, means to be moved violently, either through rage or joy. Moved or agitated is the rendering of the versions and the *Targum*. To be moved with joy is to exult or glory; and so *Blayney* renders it, and connects the end of this verse with the following, *i.e*., that the nations gloried because of the fall of Babylon, — <span style="color:#000080">Therefore shall nations glory, [saying,] Babylon is suddenly fallen,</span> <span style="color:#000080">etc. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH85</span> Another view has been given of this clause: It is a mode of speaking to express the greatness of a thing; see Psalm 36:5; Ezra 9:6. The judgement or punishment of Babylon would be so great, that

<!-- PAGE 1343 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">it might be compared to what may reach to the heavens, and to the ethereal regions; for such is the meaning of the last word, and is rendered “stars,” by the *Sept*., — <span style="color:#000080">For to the heavens has reached her judgement,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And it has risen up to the ethereal regions.</span></p>

By “heavens,” are often meant the skies. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH86</span> The second clause in the versions and the *Targ*. is, “Fill the quivers,” *i.e*., with arrows. But the word means “shields:” hence some render the verb in the sense of filling up or completing. “Complete the shields,” *i.e*., their number, or rather, more consistently with sharpening or polishing the arrows, “Fill up,” or mend, “the shields.” So *Venema* and *Parkhurst*. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH87</span> Though the Hebrew here is exactly the same as in Jeremiah 10, except that “Israel” is omitted here, yet the *Vulg*., the *Syr*., and the *Targ*. give a different version; but in the *Sept*. it is the same. But many copies have “israel” here, which is no doubt the correct reading. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH88</span> Many render this passage in the future tense, according to all the Versions and the *Targ*., and consider Cyrus to be intended by the “hammer;” but they render <span style="color:#008080">˚b</span>, *by*, or *with thee*, contrary to the *Targ*. and the Versions, which is rendered *in thee, i.e*., Babylon; and *for thee* in the *Syr*. And this seems to be the view most consistent with the whole passage, especially Jeremiah 51:24. Babylon was the “hammer” which God had employed, “Jeremiah 50:23) but he would hereafter employ, as it were, a hammer, or a scatterer, in Babylon itself, — <span style="color:#000080">20. A scatterer (or a hammer) art thou to me, A weapon of war;</span> <span style="color:#000080">But I will scatter in thee nations, And destroy in thee kingdoms;</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">21. And I will scatter in thee the horse and its rider, And I will</span> <span style="color:#000080">scatter in thee the chariot and its rider;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">22. And I will scatter in thee the husband and the wife, And I will</span> <span style="color:#000080">scatter in thee the old and the child, And I will scatter in thee the</span> <span style="color:#000080">young man and the maid;</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1344 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">23. And I will scatter in thee the shepard and his flock, And I will</span> <span style="color:#000080">scatter in thee the plougman and his team, And I will scatter in thee</span> <span style="color:#000080">the governors and princes.</span></p>

The comes, naturally, a summary of the whole, — <span style="color:#000080">24. And I will render to Babylon And to all the inhabitants of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Chaldea, All the evil which they have done in Sion, Before your</span> <span style="color:#000080">eyes, saith Jehova.</span>

The in the two following verse Babylon is still addressed.

“Scatter” is according to the *Sept*., the *Syr*., and the *Targ*.; “dash against one another” is the *Vulg*. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH89</span> *Blayney* views “the mountain” differently, as a metaphor for a nation, or a prince, rising above others in power: and “the rocks” he considers to be the strongholds of this mountain. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH90</span> Though the reatest part of the walls and towers was built of bricks, yet there were stones no doubt used. Some understand, by “stone,” a king or prince, and consider that an intimation is given that Babylon would not hereafter have a king or its own, but be tributary. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH91</span> The Version and the *Targ*. all differ as to the word <span style="color:#008080">rspf</span>, rendered by *Calvin*, “leader.” It is translated “commander” by *Blayney. Parkhurst* says that it is a Chaldee word, from <span style="color:#008080">spf</span>, to reduce to order, and <span style="color:#008080">rs</span>, a ruler. Then it means a commanding officer, a caption, or a general. It occurs only here and in Nahum 3:17. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH92</span> The Hebrew is, “the kings of Media;” but the *Sept*. and *Syr*. have “the king of Media;” which is required by “his dominion” at the end of the verse: the pronoun affixed to “captains” and “rulers” is “her,” referring to Media. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH93</span> The “earth” here is evidently the land of Chaldea or Babylon, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And tremble shall the land and be in pain; For confirmed respecting</span> <span style="color:#000080">Babylon shall be the purposes of Jehovah, To set the land of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Babylon a waste, Without an inhabitant. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH94</span> It seems to have been taken at its two extremities: hence the runners met each other at the king’s palace, from both ends of the city, and each said, that it was taken at its end. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1345 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftH95</span> The word <span style="color:#008080">µymga</span>, properly pools, is probably a metonomy for what they grow, even reeds or bulrushes, especially as the same word, in somewhat another form, <span style="color:#008080">ˆmga</span>, clearly means a reed. See Isaiah 9:14; Isaiah 19:15. But what these reeds were, authors are at a loss to know. It is said in the thirtieth verse, that they “burnt her habitations;” may it not have been, that they were such as were made of reeds? Then the whole verse appears intelligible; the passages (that is, the entrances from the river, whose streams were diverted) were seized on, and such houses as were in part built of reeds were set on fire; hence the men were frightened. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH96</span> By identifying the time of threshing and the time of harvest, it is that we can see the meaning  of this verse. Mention is first made of threshing or treading — the punishment prepared for Babylon; then it is said that what led to that — the harvest, would shortly come. The verb “come” is to be understood in the third line, it being given only in the last, — <span style="color:#000080">33. For thus saith Jehovah of hosts, the God of Israel, —  Babylon</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall be*</span> <span style="color:#000080">like a threshing-floor;</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Come shall*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the time of threshing her;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Yet a little while, and come to her shall the time of harvest.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The order as to the threshing and harvest is similar to what is often found in the prophets, — the last thing, being the main thing, is mentioned first, and then what precedes or leads to it. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH97</span> The pronoun after the verbs in this verse is in the plural number, *us*, according to the present Hebrew text, but according to the *Keri* and several copies, it is in the singular number, *me*. The authority as to MSS. is nearly equal; only the latter reading is favored by the versions and the *Targ*, and also by the verse which follows. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH98</span> The common meaning of the verb is, violently to disturb, but it is evidently used in the sense of breaking, crushing, or breaking in pieces, in Isaiah 28:28; and this is the most suitable sense here, as it follows “devouring.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH99</span> Or a sea-monster, or a whale, who devours smaller fish whole and entire. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1346 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftH100</span> The last verb is left out by the *Sept*., rendered “cast out,” by the *Vulg*., “destroyed,” by the *Syr*.; “made to emigrate,” by the *Targ*. The verb properly means to drive out or away; and their ejection from the land is what is meant. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH101</span> Taking this verse is connection with the following, *Gataker* and *Lowth* give somewhat another view, — that the Babylonians roared like lions and shouted with exultation *before* the city was taken. It is said by *Herodotus*, that “they ascended the walls, and capered, and loaded Darius and his army with reproaches.” They roared with rage at their enemies, and excited themselves as whelps when beginning to hunt for themselves, full of life and animation, — <span style="color:#000080">Together as young lions shall they roar. And rouse themselves as</span> <span style="color:#000080">whelps of lionesses.</span>

There is a <span style="color:#008080">w</span> wanting before the last verb, which is supplied by the *Vulg*., *Syr*., and the *Targ*.; and it is rendered necessary by the tense of the verb. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH102</span> “In their heat,” that is, as it appears, of rage, while they were roaring like lions. The word rendered “feasts” by *Calvin* and in our version, properly means drinking, and it is so rendered in the early versions, and more suitably here, — <span style="color:#000080">In their heat I will set</span> <span style="color:#000080">*for them*</span> <span style="color:#000080">their drink, And will make them</span> <span style="color:#000080">drunk, that they may leap for joy; And they shall sleep a perpetual</span> <span style="color:#000080">sleep, And shall not awake, saith Jehovah.</span>

It is a clear allusion to the feast celebrated in Babylon the very night it was taken. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH103</span> The *Sept*. and the *Syr*. remove the incongruity that is in this verse; they supply <span style="color:#008080">k</span> before the “land” that occurs first, and omit the second “land.” Then the verse would read thus, — <span style="color:#000080">43. Become have her cities a desolation, Like a land of drought and</span> <span style="color:#000080">a wilderness; Dwell in them shall no man, And pass through them</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall no son of man.</span>

The second “land” is omitted in two MSS.; and one has “in her,” instead of “in them.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH104</span> “The long processions of pilgrims,” observes *Henderson*, “moving slowly along, are fitly expressed by <span style="color:#008080">rhn</span>, which properly signifies, *to*

<!-- PAGE 1347 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">*flow as a river*.” — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH105</span> Some, as *Blayney*, following the *Syr*., connect this verse with the preceding: The Jews are bidden to leave Babylon, that they might escape the wrath of God, and lest their hearts should faint at the evil romors that would spread there, — <span style="color:#000080">And lest your heart faint, And ye be afraid of the rumor rumored in</span> <span style="color:#000080">the land, —  For it shall come in one year, the romor, etc.</span>

But if <span style="color:#008080">ˆp</span>, rendered *lest*, be taken, as it is sometimes, a dissuasive particle, then the rendering would be as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">And let not your heart be faint, Nor be ye afraid of the rumor</span> <span style="color:#000080">rumored in the land; When it shall come in one year, the rumor,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And afterwards in a year, the rumor, And violence</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall be*</span> <span style="color:#000080">in the</span> <span style="color:#000080">land, ruler against ruler.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The reference seems to be to the commotions in Babylon before the liberation of the Jews. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH106</span> Rather,

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And all her slain, they shall fall in the midst of her. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH107</span> This verse may be deemed as the shouting song at the fall of Babylon, — <span style="color:#000080">“As Babylon</span> <span style="color:#000080">*made*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to fall the slain of Israel, So for Babylon have</span> <span style="color:#000080">fallen the slain of all the land.”</span>

It is said before, in Jeremiah 50:47, that her slain should fall in the midst of her land. “For Babylon” means, on account of what she had done. But if it be “in Babylon,” means, on account of what she had done. But of Babylon; and the intimation is, that there would be none led captive, but slain in the land, except “all” be taken, as is often the case, as signifying a large number. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH108</span> The idea seems to be, if Babylon ascended the heavens, or the skies, and fortified there a high place for her strength, yet to this place desolators would come, — <span style="color:#000080">Though Babylon mounted the skies, And though she fortified the</span> <span style="color:#000080">height</span> <span style="color:#000080">*as*</span> <span style="color:#000080">her strength, From me would come to her destroyers,</span> <span style="color:#000080">saith Jehovah. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1348 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftH109</span> This is the meaning given by the *Targum. Venema* and *Horsley* would put a colon or a period after <span style="color:#008080">dba</span>, —

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">55. For Jehovah is laying waste Babylon and destroying her: From</span> <span style="color:#000080">her comes a loud voice! And roar do their waves like great waters,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Going forth is the tumult of their voice.</span></p>

According to the preceding verse, the destruction of Babylon is represented as *then* taking place, — <span style="color:#000080">54. A voice of howling from Babylon! And of great destruction</span> <span style="color:#000080">from the land of the Chaldeans!</span>

The commotions and tumults, arising from the invasion of enemies, seem to be set forth in Jeremiah 51:55; and the beginning of the following, Jeremiah 51:56, ought to be rendered in the present tense, the first verb being a participle. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH110</span> The <span style="color:#008080">w</span> before <span style="color:#008080">w[gy</span> is evidently conversive, and may be rendered *so that*, or *therefore*, — <span style="color:#000080">Thus saith Jehovah of hosts, The wall of Babylon, the brroad one,</span> <span style="color:#000080">It shall be utterly laid in ruins; And her gates, the lofty ones, They</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall be consumed with fire: So that people had labored for vanity,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And nations for the fire, and wearied themselves.</span>

Several MSS. have <span style="color:#008080">tmj</span>, wall, and so it is in the *Sept*., as required by “broad,” which is in the singular number. “For vanity” is for the vain object; and “for the fire” means for what was to be consumed by fire. The last words may be rendered “though they wearied themselves.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH111</span> The *Vulg*. and *Syr*. have “with,” but the *Sept*. and *Targ*. give it the meaning of “from;” and <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> has often the meaning of <span style="color:#008080">tam</span>; see Genesis 6:1; Genesis 44:4; Genesis 50:9, 29. So

*Gataker, Venema*, and *Blayney*. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH112</span> The variety in the early versions is remarkable; the *Sept*. and the *Targ*. have “the prince of gifts” or presnts; the *Vulg*., “the prince of prophecy;” and the *Syr*., “the prince of warfare.” A similar phrase is found in 1 Chronicles 22:9; Solomon is said to be “a man of rest,” <span style="color:#008080">çya</span> <span style="color:#008080">hjwnm</span>. The meaning most suitable to this passage is that if

<!-- PAGE 1349 -->

*Calvin* and of our version. So though *Gataker*; but *Lowth* and *Parkhurst* regarded the words as pointing out his office as the king’s chief chamberlain, “the prince of the resting-place,” or chamber; but the objection to this is, that the word is never used in this sense; it means not the rest of sleep, but the rest of peace and quietness. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH113</span> That the connection may appear more evident, Jeremiah 51:60 and the first sentence in Jeremiah 61:61 ought to be put within a parenthesis; for “the word which Jeremiah commanded Seraiah,” mentioned in Jeremiah 51:59, is what follows, “When thou comest to Babylon,” etc. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH114</span> Literally the words are, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">For desolations of perpetuity shall it (or she) be.</span></p>

Babylon is sometimes referred to as masculine, and sometimes as feminine. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">fth115</span> Calvin takes no notice here of the verb which closes this sentence, <span style="color:#008080">wp[yw</span>; but in his version he renders it, “and they shall fly,” or they shall be wearied. Critics know not what to make of it: it is omitted in the *Sept*., and rendered by the *Vulg*., “and it shall be dissolved;” by the *Syr*., “but they shall be thrown down;” and by the *Targ*., “and they shall fail.” It is left out in no MS. *Blayney*, following the *Sept*., omits it. The best explanation is given by *Junius* and *Tremelius*, “though they may weary themselves,” that is, the citizens of Babylon: their attempt to rise and resist their enemies would be ineffectual, however much they might toil in the effort.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The emendator, *Houbigant*, proposes to read the word, <span style="color:#008080">wpsyw</span>, “and they shall come to an end.” This agrees nearly with the *Targ*., “and they shall fail.” — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftH116</span> The best rendering of this verse is by Venema, —

Therefore the height of the wrath of Jehovah (that is, the extreme wrath of Jehovah) was on Jerusalem and Judah, until he cast them from his presence: and Zedekiah rebelled against the king of Babylon.

The same words, in 2 Kings 24:20, ought to be rendered in the same way. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH117</span> Literally, “in the house of visitations,” that is, of punishments. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1350 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftH118</span> It is “the seventh” in 2 Kings 25:8. This discrepancy is accounted for by *Blayney* and others, by suppposing a typographical mistake of putting the numeral <span style="color:#008080">z</span>, seven, for <span style="color:#008080">y</span>, ten,  or *vice versa*. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH119</span> It is <span style="color:#008080">db[</span>, “served,” in 2 Kings 25:8; but the meaning is the same. To stand before one is phrase which designates the office of one who serves. See Luke 1:29. It would be better, in rendering this part of the verse, to change the place of the verb, to come, — “Nebuzar-adan, the prince of the executioners, who stood before the king of Babylon, came to Jerusalem.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH120</span> The literal rendering is, “even every great house burnt he with fire.” This clause qualifies the former one. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH121</span> Though in the parallel passages in Jeremiah 39:9, and in 2 Kings 25:11, the words, “Some of the poor of the people,”

ar3e not found, yet the *Vulg*., the *Syr*., and the *Targ*., retain them here, and they are found in all the MSS. except one. Some of the poor of the city were evidently left, as well as some of the poor of the land. In the following words, the *Vulg*. for the most part is followed, which is not correct: the words should be, “et reliquum populum,” and, “et reliquias multitudinis.” And the past words should be, “even the remnant (or remainder) of the multitude,” that is, of the people that formerly inhabited the city. See Jeremiah 39:9. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH122</span> “Which gold, gold, and which silver, silver:” <span style="color:#008080">rça</span>, “which,” repeated, may be rendered here, “some;” and the repetition of “gold,” and of “silver,” is the same as to say, that some of the vessels were pure gold, and that some were pure silver: then the version would be this, “some gold of gold,” *i.e*., pure gold, “and some silver of silver,” *i.e*., pure silver, there being no mixture in either. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftH123</span> The number here given is to be restricted to the years here specified, that is, the *seventh*, the *eighteenth*, and the *twenty-third* of Nebuchadnezzar’s reign. We read of other captives; that is, in the *third* year of Jehoiakim’s reign, (Daniel 1:1) which was the *first* of Nebuchadnezzar; and in the *eighth* year of his reign, (2 Kings 24:12) when Jehoiachin was taken prisoner, and with him not less than ten thousand people, (2 Kings 24:14.)

<!-- PAGE 1351 -->
We have no account of the number in the first captivity, when Daniel was taken to Babylon. The largest number in the *eighth* year of Nebuchadnezzar’s reign, even *ten* thousand. The amount of *three* captivities mentioned here, the last of which must have been after the murder of Gedaliah, is *four* thousand *six* hundred. All these being men, and of full age, there must have been many women and children. It has been thought that all, taken captive all these times, could not have been less than fifty thousand. — *Ed*.
